Every search response, /api/status and the /ws/progress frames carry index so clients can say so:

FieldMeaning
watchingA file watcher applies edits, renames and deletes as they happen.
synced_at_msWhen the last full scan of the indexed folders started (Unix ms). Every change made before then is in the index. Absent until the first build or reconcile finishes.
updated_at_msWhen the watcher last changed the index (Unix ms).

GET /api/status?paths=src/a.rs;src/new.rs also compares each path (at most 100, separated by ;, as results print them, relative to an indexed folder, or absolute) with the disk and returns "paths": [{"path": …, "state": …}]. state is one of the following:

StateMeaning
currentThe file’s modification time and size match what was indexed.
staleThe file changed after it was indexed.
deletedStill in the index but gone from disk.
not_indexedOn disk but not in the index: written after the last sync, or excluded by the configuration.
not_foundNeither in the index nor under an indexed folder.
unknownIndexed, but its indexed version was not recorded.

When watching is false, the web UI shows “Index synced 6 min ago · watching off” beside the search time, fcs prints the same warning on stderr, and MCP search_code ends with it. Updating the index on request (for example right after an agent writes a file) is tracked in #140.

Query syntax

Plain-text queries (not regex=true) are a literal, like rg -F: the query minus its operators is searched for as written.

SyntaxMeaning
fn mainThe phrase fn main, exactly one space. Several words are one phrase.
"fn main", -WallQuotes and a leading - are part of the text.
file:PATTERN / -file:PATTERNOnly / never paths matching the glob; a bare word matches anywhere in the path, src/ means everything under src.
lang:rust / -lang:pyOnly / never files of that language.
case:yesCase-sensitive.
word:yesWhole words only.

Ranking

Each hit’s score combines the match with structural signals (weights in src/search/ranking.rs):

SignalEffect
Line defines a symbol3×
Exact case2×
Match at the start of the line1.5×
File under src/ or lib/1.5×
Files that import this file1 + 0.5·log10(dependents)
Long lineinverse-length damping
Test or example path (fast mode)0.7×

Results are in rank order with each file’s hits kept together: files in the order of their best hit, then every hit of that file by score.

Import graph

The dependency explorer (/graph.html) is built on these. Every endpoint answers from memory under the engine read lock. file accepts a display path (project/src/main.rs) or any path /api/file accepts. Unknown files are 404.

Rust mod foo; declarations are edges from a parent module to its own child. They describe structure, not dependencies, and would put every parent and child in a cycle, so they are left out unless containment=true (also accepted by every endpoint below). A pub use child::X re-export is the same edge, so it is left out too.

EndpointParametersReturns
GET /api/graph/neighborhoodfile, depth (1–4, default 2), limit (files per level, default 12, max 500), expand (comma-separated levels to list up to 500 files, e.g. -2,1)nodes (path, signed depth: negative for files it imports, positive for files importing it; imports, imported_by, test, cycle), edges among them (from, to, cycle, containment), hidden (per level: count and the shared folder or number of folders), and upstream / downstream totals
GET /api/graph/impactfile, limit, expandEvery file that transitively imports file, by distance: affected, levels, tests_total, tests (up to 200, nearest first), plus nodes / edges / hidden as above
GET /api/graph/pathfrom, toThe shortest import chain as files (each imports the next). When there is none from from to to, the reverse chain with reversed: true; found: false when neither exists
GET /api/graph/modules—The graph collapsed to folders: folders (files, imports, imported_by, cycle) and edges (from, to, count of file imports, cycle); unresolved_imports, the imports that resolve to no indexed file, and unresolved_local, those of them written as a path into the project (./x, .x, crate::, @/), which point at a broken import or a form fcs does not resolve. Cached until the index changes
GET /api/graph/importsfileThe file’s import statements: line (1-based), spec as written, the indexed target it resolves to (null for packages, the standard library and files outside the index), containment
GET /api/graph/filesq (case-insensitive substring), limit (default 100, max 1000)files most connected first, with imports / imported_by, and the total that matched

Imports are resolved for Rust (crate::, self::, super::, sibling modules and the crate’s own name), Python and JavaScript/TypeScript.

POST /api/impact

For each function, class, method, type or constant a unified diff changes, the files that use it. The body is JSON:

FieldDefaultMeaning
diffrequiredA unified diff, as git diff prints it (8 MiB at most)
rootnoneThe folder the diff’s paths are relative to, on the server’s machine. Without it, paths are matched as display paths, then as unique path suffixes
max_files200Files listed per changed symbol (0 = all)
max_lines3Use lines listed per file (0 = all)

The response:

  • files: each file in the diff, with status (modified, added, deleted, renamed), the indexed_path it was matched to, how many changed symbols it holds, module_lines (changed lines outside every definition, not traced) and a note when it was not analysed (not indexed, or the indexed copy matches neither side of the diff).
  • symbols: per changed symbol, name, qualname (Class.method), kind (function, class, type, constant, method, field), change (added, modified, removed), signature (a changed line is in its signature), line, the names searched (renames found are added), scope (importers, all_importers for members, repository for a language without an import graph, none), total_files, tiers (reference, code, text file counts), users and notes.
  • Each user: path, tier (its best line’s), names_owner (members: the file also names the class), in_diff, test, lines (line, column, text, tier, and the reference kind) and total_lines.
  • total_files: the distinct files using any changed symbol; notes.

Totals count every file found; users and lines are capped by max_files and max_lines, and a capped symbol has a note saying so. A body that holds no file changes is a 400. The method, what it misses and its measured accuracy: Diff impact.

MCP (coding agents)

The web server also speaks the Model Context Protocol at /mcp, so a coding agent can use the index instead of grepping: search, read files with line numbers, and walk the import graph. Point the agent at the URL:

claude mcp add --transport http fast_code_search http://127.0.0.1:8080/mcp

For clients that only launch local commands, fcs mcp relays MCP over stdio to the same endpoint (it finds the server like every fcs command: --server, $FCS_SERVER, the configuration, then http://127.0.0.1:8080):

{ "mcpServers": { "fast_code_search": { "command": "fcs", "args": ["mcp"] } } }
ToolArgumentsWhat it returns
search_codequery (the query syntax), mode (text, regex, symbols, references), include / exclude globs, limit (default 50, max 1,000: mcp.default_limit / mcp.max_limit), offset (page on from there), max_files, context (0–5); an old client’s rank is accepted and ignoredpath:line:column: line per hit, with the match count and, when more exist, the offset of the next page; in text and regex mode up to 20 files whose path matches are listed first (mcp.file_matches)
read_filepath, start_line, end_lineNumbered lines, 200 by default and at most 1,000 (mcp.read_file_lines / mcp.max_read_file_lines), with how to continue
file_dependenciespath, direction (in, out, both), depth (1–4), limitFiles it imports and files importing it, by distance, with the line of each direct import
change_impactpaths (one or more), limitEvery file that transitively imports any of them, nearest first, split into tests and other code
diff_impactdiff (unified diff), root (optional), max_files (default 50, 0 = all), max_lines (default 2)For each symbol the diff changes, the files that use it, best first, with use lines and tiers. See POST /api/impact
import_pathfrom, toThe shortest chain of imports between two files, either way round
dependency_overviewdirectory (optional), limitThe most connected folders, the heaviest folder-to-folder imports, folder cycles and hub files
index_statuspaths (optional, at most 100)When the index last synced with disk and whether file watching is on, plus, for each path, whether it is current, stale, not indexed yet or deleted. See Index freshness

Every tool is read-only and answers from the same handlers as the REST API, so it shares their search permits, deadlines and caps. Results use the root-relative paths search results print, and lists are capped with a note saying what was left out. The graph tools say which languages have import edges (Rust, Python, JavaScript/TypeScript), so an empty answer for another language is not mistaken for “unused”.

Transport details: each JSON-RPC message is a POST /mcp answered with application/json (notifications get 202); there is no event stream (GET /mcp is 405) and no session. Requests carrying a browser Origin are refused with 403 unless it is a loopback address, the server’s own address, or listed in cors_origins, so a web page cannot drive a local server through the visitor’s browser. Like the REST API, /mcp has no authentication: anyone who can reach the web port can read the index.

gRPC

gRPC has no diff impact call; use POST /api/impact, MCP diff_impact or fcs impact.

The schema is proto/search.proto: Search streams SearchResult messages with the same fields and modes as REST (is_regex, symbols_only, references, case_sensitive, whole_word, include_paths, exclude_paths, rank, offset, deadline_ms), and Index adds paths under the configured roots. The standard grpc.health.v1 service is served too. A minimal client: examples/client.rs (cargo run --example client).

Limits match REST: offset ≤ max_page_offset, fast ranking opens fast_rank_files, the search semaphore is shared with the REST side, and every search runs under a deadline derived from the request timeout.

Server command line

fast_code_search_server [OPTIONS]

  -c, --config <FILE>       Configuration file
  -a, --address <ADDR>      gRPC listen address (overrides config)
      --web-address <ADDR>  Web UI / REST listen address (overrides config)
  -i, --index <PATH>        Additional path to index (repeatable)
      --no-auto-index       Skip indexing on startup
      --no-grpc             Do not start the gRPC API (same as enable_grpc = false)
      --init <FILE>         Write a documented template configuration and exit
      --static-dir <DIR>    Serve the UI from disk (development)
  -v, --verbose             Debug logging

Configuration discovery: --config, then $FCS_CONFIG, then ./fast_code_search.toml, then ~/.config/fast_code_search/config.toml. The first log lines say which file was used (or that none was found and the defaults apply) and any warnings about it.

If one of the ports cannot be bound, the server logs which port and keeps running on the other API; it exits only when neither could start. The diagnostics page and /api/health report the failure.

Configuration file

fast_code_search_server --init config.toml writes a fully commented template. The keys that matter most:

[server]
address = "127.0.0.1:50051"       # gRPC
enable_grpc = true                # false (or --no-grpc) for web UI / REST / fcs only
web_address = "127.0.0.1:8080"    # REST + web UI
enable_web_ui = true
max_concurrent_searches = 64      # shared by REST and gRPC; beyond it: 503 / RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED
request_timeout_secs = 30         # searches get an engine deadline just under this
fast_rank_files = 2000            # files fast ranking opens by default (0 = all)
max_fast_rank_files = 0           # most a request may ask for with max_files (0 = no limit)
max_page_offset = 100000          # deepest offset a client may page to (0 = no limit)
cors_origins = []                 # e.g. ["https://ide.example.com"]; empty = same-origin only

[indexer]
paths = ["~/work", "/srv/repos/other"]     # ~ expands; relative paths resolve against this file
exclude_patterns = ["**/node_modules/**", "**/target/**", "**/.git/**"]
include_extensions = []           # empty = every text file; ["rs", "py"] to restrict
max_file_size = 10485760          # bytes; 0 = default
respect_gitignore = true
show_root_name = true             # false: src/main.rs instead of myapp/src/main.rs (one path only)

index_path = "~/.local/share/fast_code_search/index.fcsidx"   # persist across restarts
save_after_build = true
checkpoint_interval_files = 20000
watch = true                      # follow edits, renames, deletes
save_after_updates = 100          # save after this many watched file changes

--init writes every key with its default, and sets index_path from the template’s own name (--init work.toml gives ~/.local/share/fast_code_search/work.fcsidx). Without index_path the index lives only in memory and is rebuilt on every start; the startup log warns about it.

Only one server writes an index. A server holds a lock on <index_path>.lock and records its PID and addresses in <index_path>.lock.owner; a second server given the same index_path logs who owns it, loads the index read-only and never saves. Give each configuration its own index_path.

At startup and before each save the server checks free space and free inodes on the filesystem holding the index (and inodes where indexed source lives), and warns when either is low.

[diagnostics] capture = true makes the server write a JSON file whenever it is slow (a search over slow_search_secs, a lock wait over lock_wait_secs) or stuck (no progress for stall_secs), saying what it was doing; fcs diagnose summarizes them. Off by default; see the book’s Diagnostic captures.

Changing exclude patterns, extensions, the size cap or a .gitignore takes effect on the next start: files that are no longer eligible are dropped when the persisted index is reconciled.

Exit codes and errors (CLI)

fcs exits 0 when it printed at least one match, 1 when the search found nothing, and 2 on error (bad arguments, invalid regex, server error, no server and no index). See CLI.md.

Query syntax

The same syntax everywhere: the web UI’s search box, fcs, and the q parameter of the API. It applies to plain-text queries; with regex=true (fcs -e) the whole query is a regular expression instead. For how the results compare with grep, ripgrep and git grep, see Matching compared with grep and ripgrep.

The text you type is searched for as written, like rg -F. The operators below are the only exceptions; everything else, spaces, quotes and dashes included, is part of the text.

WriteTo get
fn mainLines holding the phrase fn main, with exactly one space. Several words are one phrase.
"fn main"Lines holding "fn main" with its quotes. Quotes are text: { "success": True finds that text as written.
-WallLines holding -Wall. A leading - is text too (->, -1, --flag). On the command line put such a query after --: fcs -- -Wall.
file:src/Only paths under a src directory. A bare word matches anywhere in the path (file:parser); globs are used as written (file:*.rs, file:crates/**/tests/**).
-file:vendorNever paths matching the pattern.
lang:rust, -lang:pyOnly / never files of that language (by extension: rs/rust, py/python, ts, js, go, java, …).
rdeps:src/search/ranking.rsOnly that file and the files that import it. See Search by imports.
deps:app/main.pyOnly that file and the files it imports.
case:yesMatch case exactly (the default is case-insensitive).
word:yesWhole words only.
kind:call, -kind:attributeReferences only: keep / drop references of that kind (call, type, value, attribute). See Reference kinds.

Operators can be combined: Widget file:src/ -lang:ts case:yes. Filters of different kinds narrow each other (file:src/ lang:rust is the Rust files under src/); repeating one kind widens it (lang:rust lang:py is either language, file:src/ file:lib/ either folder). They are whole words with an argument: file: alone, or url=file:x, is text. Operators between words join the words with one space, so fn file:src/ main searches for fn main under src/. The ends of the query are trimmed; spaces inside it are kept. To search for operator-looking text such as file:x, use a regex.

Reference kinds: kind:

Each reference is one of four kinds, shown on the result (in the web UI’s tag, as [call] in fcs refs, as reference_kind in the API’s JSON and as [reference: call] in MCP):

KindIsExample, for size or Item
calla call, or a decoratorx.size(), @cached
typea type or class mention: an annotation, a base class, new T, an implemented interfacedef f(x: Item), class A(Item)
valuea function or class used as a value: passed, assigned, compared, returnedisinstance(x, Item), cb = size
attributean attribute that is read, without a call: a property, a method passed alongn = other.size

refs size kind:call keeps the calls only; kind:call,type (or kind:call kind:type) keeps either; -kind:attribute keeps everything but attribute reads, the usual way to leave out properties of other classes that share the name. attr is short for attribute. Which kinds a language records is on Reference support: value and attribute are Python only for now, and every other language’s references are calls and types. kind: with a text or symbol search is an error rather than being ignored; a regex is not parsed for operators.

Search by imports: deps: and rdeps:

These limit a search to the files the import graph connects to one file, so the question “where is this used, among the code a change to this file could affect?” is one query:

WriteTo search
Weight rdeps:src/search/ranking.rsranking.rs and the files that import it
Weight rdeps:src/search/ranking.rs:2the same, up to two imports away
Weight rdeps:src/search/ranking.rs:*everything that reaches ranking.rs, at any distance
config deps:app/main.pymain.py and the files it imports (:N and :* as above, 10 at most)
Weight rdeps:src/search/every file under the folder, and the files that import any of them
Weight -rdeps:src/search/ranking.rseverything except those files
  • The path is the file’s path as results show it, or any trailing part of it that names one file (rdeps:ranking.rs when only one file ends that way). A path that matches no file, or several, is an error that lists them; so is a file in a language the graph does not cover (edges exist for Rust, Python and JavaScript/TypeScript, see Dependency graph support).
  • Combining: repeating rdeps: (or deps:) searches the union; a deps: and an rdeps: together search the files in both; both narrow file: and lang:, and they narrow each other.
  • Package entry files are passed through, carefully. An import of a package usually lands on its entry file (mod.rs, lib.rs, main.rs, __init__.py, index.ts/index.js), not on the module behind it. So an edge from an entry file to a module of its own package does not count as a hop, and a file reached across one is kept only if it names something the module on the other side defines (a function, type, constant or macro as a whole word, or the module itself in a path such as ranking:: or ./ranking). rdeps:src/search/ranking.rs therefore finds the files that use crate::search::RankingWeights, but not every importer of crate::search. Only one entry file is crossed in a row (ranking.rs to search/mod.rs, never on to lib.rs for free). A child module that imports its parent (Rust use super::*) is an ordinary hop: use :2.
  • Negated filters walk strictly. -rdeps: and -deps: follow import edges only, with no pass-through, so they never leave out a file that does not depend on the anchor.
  • Rust paths without a use (crate::search::fold::fold_str(…), super::util::Thing) count as imports of the module they name.
  • The scope is only as complete as the graph. The results header (and the scope object of the API, MCP’s text, fcs’s summary) says how many files were searched, and warns when imports in those files are written as project paths (./x, crate::x) but resolve to no file: the scope may be missing whatever they point at.
  • Ranking: nearer files rank a little higher; each hit’s hops (0 for the file itself and its package entry) is in the API response and the row’s tooltip.
  • Everywhere: in the query on any surface; for a regex, whose query is all pattern, as the deps / rdeps API parameters (semicolon-separated, a leading - to leave out), fcs --deps PATH[:N] / --rdeps, MCP search_code’s deps / rdeps, gRPC’s deps / rdeps fields. In the web UI they are the FILTER panel’s Files that import and Files imported by boxes, and the dependency popover and the explorer’s Search importers / Search its imports buttons fill them in.

Inside the scope, matching is exactly what it is without one: a scoped search finds what ripgrep finds over the same files.

Case

Plain text ignores case unless the query says case:yes. A regex matches case unless it starts with (?i) or the request says to ignore it. The web UI’s MATCH CASE toggle (case on the API, fcs -s / -i, MCP case_sensitive) sets it for every mode: off, upper and lower case are the same in plain text and regex alike; on, they are matched exactly. The results header always says which applied (“ignoring case” or “matching case”).

Modes

ModeUI / CLI / APIReturns
Text (default)— / fcs / —Lines matching the query, ranked
RegexREGEX / fcs -e / regex=trueRust regex syntax, matched one line at a time. To span lines, mention \n or set (?s)
SymbolsSYMBOLS / fcs symbols / symbols=trueDefinitions only (functions, types, classes, constants, …) plus filename matches
ReferencesREFERENCES / fcs refs / references=trueCall sites and type mentions of the identifier

References are exact and case-sensitive on the identifier; file: and lang: still narrow the files, and kind: the kinds of use. Which positions count as a reference differs by language, and C and C++ record none: see Reference support.

Regex notes

  • Literals in the pattern pre-filter through the trigram index; a pattern with no required literal of three or more characters scans every file.
  • ^ and $ anchor at line boundaries (CRLF included); \s does not cross a line break unless the pattern is a multi-line one.
  • On the API and fcs -e, a regex is case-sensitive unless it starts with (?i) or the request says case=false (fcs -e -i). In the web UI it follows MATCH CASE, like plain search.
  • { } ( ) [ ] . * + ? | ^ $ \ are syntax; escape one with \ to match it (\{, \.). For code exactly as written, search without regex.
  • Spaces are exact: \{ "a" misses {"a". Use \s* for any amount of space, including none.
  • In the web UI the ? next to REGEX has runnable examples, and a search that finds nothing offers close variants that do.
  • Patterns are compiled with size limits; an absurd one is rejected with a 400 rather than run.

How matching compares with grep, ripgrep and git grep

fcs aims to find exactly the lines ripgrep finds for the same search. Every difference listed below is either deliberate, or the result of fcs being an index rather than a scan tool. ripgrep is the reference because it is the closest well-known tool: the same regex engine (Rust regex), Unicode-aware case folding, and .gitignore handling. The ripgrep backtest checks this on every engine change. It runs a fixed set of searches through both tools and fails on any line one finds and the other does not.

Every search, as ripgrep would run it

The contract in one table: for each kind of fcs search, the ripgrep run that finds the same lines. “Same lines” is what the backtest checks; fcs then ranks them and may cap how many it reads (see Ranked, and capped below, and limits_hit in every response).

fcs searchSame lines asChecked by
foo bar (plain text)rg -F -i 'foo bar'backtest, generated queries
foo case:yes (or MATCH CASE, fcs -s)rg -F 'foo'backtest, generated queries
foo word:yes (or fcs -w)rg -F -i -w 'foo'backtest, generated queries
foo case:yes word:yesrg -F -w 'foo'backtest, generated queries
Regex on the API or fcs -erg 'PATTERN' (rg -U when it mentions \n)backtest
Regex with case=false, (?i) or fcs -e -irg -i 'PATTERN'backtest
lang:rust foorg -F -i -t rust 'foo'backtest (with globs)
file:*.py foo / -file:tests foorg -F -i -g '*.py' 'foo' / -g '!tests'; a bare word such as file:parser matches anywhere in the path, which -g has no exact form forbacktest (with globs)
file:src/ lang:rust foorg -F -i -g 'src/**/*.rs' 'foo': different filters narrow each other, as -g and -t dobacktest (with globs)
rdeps:src/a.rs foo / deps:rg -F -i 'foo' FILE… over the files the import graph puts in scope; rg has no graphbacktest (rg’s hits kept to the scope’s files)
A text search’s filename hits (no line number)none; closest is rg --files | rg -i 'foo'not compared
Path matches (files=N, the FILES box)none; closest is rg --files | rg -i with every word requiredunit tests
Symbols (SYMBOLS, fcs symbols)none: definitions come from tree-sitter, not from textengine tests
References (REFERENCES, fcs refs)none: call sites come from tree-sitter, not from textengine tests

fcs on the command line, the web UI, the API, gRPC and MCP all run one shared request path, and a test runs the same searches through web, gRPC and offline fcs and requires identical hits (tests/frontend_parity.rs).

Where fcs agrees with ripgrep and differs from grep and git grep

BehaviourfcsripgrepGNU grepgit grep
Regex dialectRust regexRust regex (PCRE2 with -P)POSIX BRE/ERE (-E), PCRE with -PPOSIX BRE/ERE, PCRE with -P
Byte order mark at the start of a fileignored, so ^ matches line 1ignored (kept with -E none)part of line 1, so ^ misses itpart of line 1, so ^ misses it
$ on a line ending in CRLFmatches before the \ronly with --crlfnono
A class such as [^a-z] or \s at the end of a CRLF linedoes not match the \ronly with --crlfmatches itmatches it
Pattern that mentions \nruns over the whole file and reports the line where the match startsneeds -Unot supportednot supported
Case-insensitive foldingUnicode simple case folding: Σ, σ and ς are the same letter, so are s and the long ſ, k and the Kelvin sign K, the micro sign µ and μ; ß is not ss and İ is not ithe samedepends on the localedepends on the locale
Hidden files (.github/, .env.example)searchedskipped unless --hiddensearchedsearched if tracked
Files searchedthe indexed tree, honouring .gitignorethe tree, honouring .gitignorewhat you nametracked files only

These were checked with GNU grep 3.11, git 2.43 and ripgrep 14.1.

Where fcs differs from all of them

These follow from fcs being a code search server rather than a line filter.

  • Case-insensitive by default. Plain text ignores case unless the query says case:yes; grep, git grep and ripgrep match case by default (rg -i is the fcs default, rg -s is case:yes). On the API and in fcs a regex matches case unless it uses (?i) or the request says case=false; in the web UI the MATCH CASE toggle decides for both. The backtest passes -i to ripgrep whenever fcs ignores case.
  • Operators. Plain text is a literal, like rg -F: several words are one phrase, and quotes and a leading - are part of the text. The only exceptions are whole words with an argument: file:, -file:, lang:, -lang:, deps:, rdeps: (and their - forms), case: and word:. To search for one of those literally, use a regex. The query is trimmed at both ends (rg ' x' keeps its leading space). See Query syntax.
  • File names match too. A text search also lists files whose name matches, as a hit without a line number. grep has no equivalent, so the backtest leaves these out of the comparison.
  • Paths match, separately. When asked (files=N on /api/search, the web UI’s FILES box, fcs on a terminal, MCP search_code), the files whose path matches are returned apart from the content results, which do not change. Here the text is split into words, and every word must appear in the path as results show it, case-insensitively unless case:yes; \ counts as /; word:yes, file:, lang: and include/exclude globs apply as they do to content. A term holding a / also matches the file’s absolute path, so a pasted /home/me/repo/src/main.rs works, but only when the match reaches past the indexed folder (/home/ alone lists nothing). A regex is matched against the shown path. This is closest to rg --files | rg PATTERN; it is not part of the backtest, which compares content only.
  • One result per line. A line with several matches is one result. For a multi-line match, the result is the line where it starts. ripgrep counts lines the same way (rg -c).
  • Ranked, and capped. Results come back best first rather than in file order. To keep a broad search fast, a request reads a bounded number of matches: a budget of 32,768 for every page that ends before result 1000, and 100,000 for every page from there on. One file contributes at most its best 1,000 hits (by score, not its first 1,000 lines) before result 1000, and up to the budget after it. Files are read best first, so the budget keeps the hits of the most likely files. Because every page in each range ranks the same sample, paging with offset (MCP’s search_code, fcs --offset, the API) returns each hit once, with no repeats or gaps; only the switch at result 1000 can differ, and only for a search that hit its budget. When a limit is reached, the total is shown as “N+” rather than as a count, and the response names the limit (limits_hit: match_budget, per_file_cap, timeout, max_files or indexing; fcs prints it, MCP adds a “Results capped” line, gRPC sends an fcs-limits-hit header). indexing means the server was still building or reconciling its index, so files not indexed yet could not match. A server may also cap the files read (search.fast_rank_files, off by default); the header then says “Searched N of M files” and SEARCH ALL FILES opens the rest (up to search.max_fast_rank_files). Symbol search reads the files holding the best-matching symbols (at least 200); when there are more, it says so in the same way, and SEARCH ALL FILES, MCP’s max_files or rank=full read them all. LOAD MORE in the web UI fetches the whole list again at the bigger size, up to 1000 results (search.max_page_size), so every match ends up shown exactly once. Results already on screen can move when it does. Past 1000 results it fetches the next page instead. Paging stops at offset 100,000 by default (search.max_page_offset). ripgrep has none of these limits; each is reported when it applies, and Search limits gives the measurements behind the defaults.
  • Filters need text. A query made only of filters (lang:rust, file:///etc) is answered with an error saying there is no text to find, not an empty result. case: and word: take yes or no (also y/n, true/false, 1/0, on/off); with any other argument the token is text, so word:count searches for word:count.
  • Non-UTF-8 files are converted. Files in Latin-1, Windows-1252, Shift-JIS or UTF-16 are converted to UTF-8 when they are indexed (transcode_non_utf8, on by default). ripgrep only does this for UTF-16 with a byte order mark, or when given -E. grep and git grep match raw bytes. A file with a NUL byte in its first 8 KB is binary and not indexed (ripgrep’s test is the same NUL byte, but it looks wherever it reads, so a NUL further in makes rg stop at it while fcs searches the whole file). Files with these extensions are not read at all: images (png, jpg, gif, ico, bmp, webp), archives, fonts, office documents, media, compiled objects and bytecode (indexer.extra_binary_extensions adds more). SVG is text and is indexed.
  • Index scope. Files over max_file_size (10 MB by default) and paths matching exclude_patterns (node_modules, target, .git, build, dist, Python virtual environments) are not indexed, so they are never searched, even when git tracks them (ripgrep searches a tracked build/). Ignore files apply as in ripgrep: .gitignore, .ignore, .rgignore, .git/info/exclude and your global git excludes (core.excludesFile), with the same precedence: a rule in an .rgignore or .ignore anywhere above a file beats a .gitignore rule next to it; a folder that is ignored stays ignored whatever a rule deeper down says (!a/b/ after /a brings nothing back, as in git); and .gitignore files apply outside git repositories and above nested ones, as with rg --no-require-git. The build and the watcher’s per-file check are both compared with rg --files on random trees of ignore files (tests/discovery_oracle.rs). When the watcher sees a .gitignore, .ignore or .rgignore change, appear or go away (a branch switch, say), it re-checks every file under that folder, as a restart would. Edits to .git/info/exclude or the global excludes file are applied at the next start, since nothing under .git is watched.
  • Files that can’t be read. ripgrep prints an error for a file it cannot open and searches the rest. fcs tries such a file again for about half a minute (it is often locked for a moment by an editor, a build or a virus scanner), then leaves it out of the index and says so with every search (“1 file could not be read and is left out of the index”); the server log names it, and it is indexed again when it next changes and can be read.
  • Folders that can’t be read. ripgrep prints an error for a folder it cannot list and searches the rest; fcs does the same, and also counts the folder with every search (“1 folder could not be read, so the files in it are missing from the index”). The server log names it. The count clears at the next scan that can read it: a restart, a rescan after lost events, or the folder being renamed or created again.

Known differences

None at the moment. A known difference would be listed here, and marked known with its reason in benches/rg_backtest.toml, so the backtest still reports it without failing.

Dependency graph support

What the import graph (the dependency explorer, “imported by N”, /api/graph, MCP’s graph tools) understands, per language. An import that is not listed here produces no edge. If fcs misses an edge listed as supported, that is a bug; if it misses one listed as not handled, that is a known gap. Python and TypeScript are checked against their own resolvers (see the end of the page). How the import graph is built explains the method, including how an import is judged internal or external.

Edges exist for Rust, Python and JavaScript/TypeScript only. Go, Java, C and C++ #include, C#, Ruby and every other language are searched and have symbols, but get no import edges.

How resolution works, for every language

  • An import resolves only to a file that is in the index. Packages outside the indexed folders (std, site-packages, node_modules, which is excluded by default) never produce an edge.
  • A bare or external name resolves to nothing, unless the language’s own configuration maps it to an indexed file (for JavaScript and TypeScript: tsconfig paths/baseUrl, a workspace package.json name, or the repository root when there is no tsconfig baseUrl). There is no “a file with that name somewhere in the repo” guess, so a missing edge is never replaced by a wrong one.
  • Imports that do not resolve are kept. They are retried when a matching file is indexed later, and GET /api/graph/imports?path=… lists every import of a file with its line, and target: null for the unresolved ones.
  • The unresolved ones are counted. Most are packages, but one written as a path into the project (./x, .x, crate::, @/) is a broken import or a form listed below as not handled. The dependency explorer’s folder map gives that count, GET /api/graph/modules returns both (unresolved_imports, unresolved_local), MCP’s dependency_overview reports them, and file_dependencies lists a file’s unresolved imports.

Rust (.rs)

FormSupported
use crate::a::b;yes, from the nearest Cargo.toml’s src/
use self::a;, use super::super::a;yes, relative to the module’s directory (foo/ for foo.rs; the file’s directory for mod.rs, lib.rs, main.rs)
use a::b; where a is a sibling moduleyes
use my_crate::a; inside my_crateyes ([lib] name, else [package] name with - as _)
use a::{b, c};, use a::*;, use a as b;yes, to the longest module path that exists (a/b.rs, then a/b/mod.rs)
mod foo;yes
crate::a::f(), super::x::Y used inline, without a useyes, once per file, like use crate::a::f
use other_workspace_crate::x;no: only the importing crate’s own name is recognised
use crate::{a, b};no: reduces to crate::, which does not reach lib.rs
extern crate x;, #[path = "…"] mod x;no
External crates and stdno edge, by design

Inline mod foo { … } is treated like mod foo;, so it can draw an edge to a file foo.rs that is not the module. Config read: Cargo.toml.

Python (.py, .pyi, .pyw)

FormSupported
import a.b, import a, b as cyes, one edge per module
from a.b import cyes: to a/b/c.py (or a/b/c/__init__.py) when c is a module, else to a/b.py or a/b/__init__.py, as Python does
from .x import y, from ..x import yyes: one dot is the file’s own package, each extra dot one level up; when x does not exist the import is unresolved, as Python fails it
from . import yyes: to y.py when it is a module, else to the package’s __init__.py
Absolute importsyes, as Python 3 does: from the ancestor folders that are not packages themselves (no __init__.py), so import json inside a package is the standard library, not a json.py next to it; where several could hold the module, the one that holds most of the dotted name wins
src/ layouts (tests/ importing src/pkg)yes: each of those folders’ src/ is tried too
Absolute imports from a file nested any number of folders deepyes: every ancestor folder is tried
A repository whose root folder has an __init__.py, run with that folder on sys.path (import app.models)yes: when nothing else resolves the import, the outermost package folder is tried as a root if it is the repository root (.git) or no folder above it holds an indexed file
Other sys.path entries (pyproject.toml, setup.cfg, PYTHONPATH)no: no configuration is read, so a package in a sibling folder (lib/mypkg/ imported from apps/x/main.py, with lib put on PYTHONPATH by your setup) is not found and the import counts as external. This includes a monorepo of projects side by side, each with its own src/ and installed into one environment (services/billing importing libs/lib_db/src/lib_db): imports within a project resolve, imports of another project do not
Namespace packages (no __init__.py)within one folder, yes; a namespace package split over several folders on sys.path (src/acme/core and plugins/src/acme/x) only where one portion’s folder is an ancestor’s src/ of the other, so imports into the other portion, absolute or relative, are mostly missed
.pyi stubs as import targetsno
Standard library, installed packagesno edge, by design

JavaScript and TypeScript (.js, .jsx, .mjs, .cjs, .ts, .tsx, .mts, .cts)

FormSupported
import … from './x', export … from './x', export * from './x'yes
require('./x'), require(`./x`), import('./x')yes (not a template string with ${…}), also import(/* comment */ './x')
import x from './x.json' with { type: 'json' }, and the older assert { … }yes
import x = require('./x') (TypeScript)yes
Relative specifiers (./, ../, ., ..)yes
@/x, ~/xyes: tsconfig paths when it maps them, else from the nearest folder with a package.json or tsconfig.json (else the repository root), trying src/x then x
Extension probingthe exact file; .js/.jsx written for a .ts/.tsx file, .mjs for .mts, .cjs for .cts; then .ts .tsx .js .jsx .mjs .cjs .mts .cts .d.ts .json appended; then index with each of those extensions. When both x.js and x.ts exist, ./x.js is x.js (as Node and bundlers load it), where TypeScript picks x.ts
Imports of stylesheets, .vue/.svelte components and other assetsyes, when the file is indexed (a bundler resolves them; TypeScript does not)
A folder’s own package.json main (import './lib' where lib/package.json names the entry)no
package.json imports (#name) and a package importing itself by nameno
tsconfig / jsconfig paths and baseUrl, with relative extendsyes, as tsc resolves them; an extends naming an npm package is skipped
Workspace packages by name (@acme/shared, @acme/shared/x)yes: to an indexed package.json with that name: its types/source/module/main/exports["."] entry, else src/index; a subpath under src/ or the package root
No tsconfig baseUrl/paths (import 'components/Button')yes: tried from the repository root (the folder holding .git)
exports subpath maps and nested conditionsno
Packages in node_modulesno edge, by design

How this is checked

Extraction and resolution have unit tests per form (src/symbols/extractor.rs, src/dependencies/mod.rs). On top of those, examples/graph_oracle.rs compares the whole graph with an independent resolver over a pinned repository and lists every edge fcs misses or draws wrongly:

LanguageOracleRepositoryResult
Pythongrimp 3.17 (src/check/oracle/py.py)Django 5.23,041 of 3,041 edges, none extra
Pythongrimpa package nested 14 levels deep; a repository whose root folder has __init__.py (scripts/graph-oracle/deep_python_tree.py)every edge, none extra
Pythongrimp, with every project’s src/ on sys.pathgenerated: services and libraries side by side, each its own project with src/ (layouts.py monorepo)368 of 698 edges, none extra; the 330 missed are imports of another project (other sys.path entries, above)
Pythongrimpgenerated: a root folder that is a package, three top-level packages importing each other (layouts.py root-init)468 of 468 edges, none extra
Pythongrimpgenerated: a namespace package split over src/ and plugins/src/ (layouts.py namespace)98 of 162 edges, none extra; the 64 missed cross from one portion to the other (namespace packages, above)
TypeScript, JavaScriptTypeScript 5.9 ts.resolveModuleName under each file’s tsconfig (src/check/oracle/ts.mjs)Vite 6.3.51,613 of 1,620 edges, 1 extra: 4 package.json imports (#name), 2 folders with their own package.json main, and one ./x.js where x.ts exists too (the extra is its other side); 221 workspace-package edges the oracle cannot see without an install are listed apart
TypeScript, JavaScriptTypeScriptgenerated: npm workspaces installed as symlinks, a root tsconfig.base.json with paths that each project extends, barrels, .mts/.cts, import type, import(), require (scripts/graph-oracle/ts_layouts.mjs)388 of 390 edges, none extra: one #name import and one exports subpath

Every edge fcs misses or draws wrongly on these is listed, with its reason and count, in benches/graph/known_gaps.toml. CI (“graph oracle”) fails when an edge is not on that list (a bug, or a gap to document here) and when a listed count changes, so a fix shows up as a gap to lower or remove. The generated repositories are written with seed 1.

python3 src/check/oracle/py.py bench-corpus/django > py.json
cargo run --release --example graph_oracle -- bench-corpus/django --oracle py.json --lang py
(cd scripts/graph-oracle && npm ci)   # pins TypeScript
(cd scripts/graph-oracle && node ../../src/check/oracle/ts.mjs ../../bench-corpus/vite) > ts.json
cargo run --release --example graph_oracle -- bench-corpus/vite --oracle ts.json --lang ts \
    --gaps benches/graph/known_gaps.toml --corpus vite
python3 scripts/graph-oracle/layouts.py monorepo py-monorepo --seed 1
(set -a; . py-monorepo/oracle.env; set +a; python3 src/check/oracle/py.py py-monorepo) > py-monorepo.json
cargo run --release --example graph_oracle -- py-monorepo --oracle py-monorepo.json --lang py \
    --gaps benches/graph/known_gaps.toml --corpus py-monorepo

To run the same comparison on your own code, use fcs check (see Correctness against ripgrep). It runs whichever oracles are installed: Python’s needs pip install grimp, TypeScript’s needs typescript in the repository’s node_modules.

Reference support

What the References mode (REFERENCES in the web UI, fcs refs, references=true, MCP mode: "references") finds, per language.

References are search-based, not compiler-based. While indexing, each file is parsed with tree-sitter, and the grammar’s tags query marks some names as references: call sites, type mentions, new expressions, base classes. A reference search returns the lines where the identifier appears in one of those positions. Two consequences hold for every language:

  • Names, not symbols. fcs refs parse returns every call of anything called parse, whichever class or module it belongs to. Nothing tells two functions with the same name apart.
  • Only the listed positions. A use in any other position (an import line; outside Python, a function passed as a value) is not a reference, even though a text search finds it. When references look incomplete, search the plain text as well.

A definition is never its own reference, and references are exact and case-sensitive on the identifier. Each reference is a call, type, value or attribute, and kind: filters on it (see Reference kinds).

If fcs misses a form listed as found, that is a bug; if it misses one listed as not found, that is a known gap.

At a glance

LanguageCallsMethod callsType mentionsnew / constructorsBase classesNot found (main gaps)
Rustyesyesyesn/aimpl Trait for Tanything inside a macro’s arguments, the Type in Type::new(), enum variants (Item::A), use lines, functions as values
Pythonyesyesyesyes (a call)yesimports, string annotations, a few value positions; also returns uses of other things with the same name (see below)
JavaScriptyesyesn/ayesnoextends, instanceof, functions as values, property reads, imports
TypeScriptyesyespartlyyesnoFoo[], Foo<T>, A | B, ns.Type, type arguments, type aliases, extends and implements, instanceof, imports
Goyesyesyescomposite literals (Item{})n/afunctions as values (Run: helper, cb := helper), field reads, imports
Javayesyespartlyyesyesfield, parameter, local and generic types, annotations, Foo.class, Foo::bar, the class in Util.f(), casts, imports
C#member calls onlyyespartlyyesyesplain calls Helper(), generic calls Make<T>(), parameter and generic types, attributes, typeof, casts, the class in Util.F(), using
Cnononon/an/aeverything: no references are recorded
C++nononononoeverything: no references are recorded
RubyyesyesconstantsItem.newyessee below: also returns local variables
PHPno (plain f())yesnonoimplements onlyplain function calls, new Item(), extends, parameter and return types, use
Bash and other languagesnonononononot parsed for references

“Partly” is spelled out in the language’s section below.

Rust (.rs)

FormFound
helper(x), self.helper(), crate::a::helper(), Type::new() (the new)yes
helper::<T>(), a::helper::<T>()yes
Types in fields, parameters, returns and generics (Result<Item, Error>), impl Trait for Syes
The Type in Type::new() or Item::default()no

Anything inside a macro call (println!("{}", helper(x)), assert!(…))no: macro arguments are not parsed
use crate::a::{Engine, helper};no
Functions as values (let cb = helper;, .map(helper))no
Field reads (self.prop)no
#[derive(Debug)]no

The macro gap matters most in tests: calls inside assert_eq!(…) are not references.

Python (.py, .pyi, .pyw)

Python is checked against Jedi, which resolves each name to its definition (see the end of the page).

FormFound
helper(x), self.helper(), pkg.util.do_thing(), Foo()yes
Decorators: @login_required, @app.route("/x"), @bp.before_app_request, @name.setteryes
Base classes: class Foo(Base, Mixin)yes
Type annotations: item: Item, -> Result, list[Item], Item | None, Callable[[A], B], t.Optional[Item]yes
Functions and classes as values: isinstance(x, Item), cb = helper, return Result, register(self.on_done), (a, b), {"k": helper}, x == helperyes
Any attribute that is read: self.prop, obj.CONSTANT, request.formyes
A class used through an attribute: Item.from_dict() (the Item, when it starts with a capital)yes
except Oops as e:, raise Failedyes
Assigning an attribute: self.prop = 1no, on purpose: it sets prop, it does not use it
A parameter or local variable with the same name as a function (def view(request, app): app.run())no, on purpose: Python makes a name bound anywhere in a function local to it, unless it is declared global or nonlocal
import, from … importno
Annotations written as strings: x: "Item"no
Other value positions: lambda bodies, yield x, *args unpacking, f-string {x}no

Each Python reference has a kind: decorators and calls are call; annotations, base classes and exceptions type; functions and classes used as values value; attribute reads attribute. -kind:attribute leaves out property reads (see Reference kinds).

Because references are names, fcs refs get also returns every .get(…) on a dictionary, and fcs refs status_code returns reads of every attribute called status_code, whichever class it belongs to. On the repositories below, between one Python reference in ten and one in five is such a use of something else with the same name.

C and C++

No references are recorded. The C and C++ grammars’ tags queries define symbols only, so fcs refs finds nothing in .c, .h, .cpp, .cc, .hpp and the other C and C++ extensions. Use a plain or whole-word text search instead.

Ruby (.rb, .rake, .gemspec)

Calls, method calls, constants (Item.new, Util.do_thing), base classes and include Mixin are found. Ruby’s tags query is meant to skip local variables, but fcs does not apply that filter, so local variables and parameters are returned as references too (x in x = Item.new).

PHP (.php, .phtml)

FormFound
$this->helper(), Util::doThing() (the doThing)yes
implements IFaceyes
Plain calls: helper($e)no
new Item()no
extends Base, parameter and return types, use linesno

How complete this is

Python, against Jedi

examples/ref_oracle.rs compares the references fcs records in a repository with the ones Jedi 0.20 finds (src/check/oracle/py_refs.py): every name Jedi resolves to a function, class or property defined in the repository, other than the definition itself and import lines. A reference Jedi lists and fcs does not is missing. A reference fcs records, under the name of one of those functions or classes, where Jedi resolves the name to something else, is extra: almost always a use of something else with the same name. Where Jedi cannot resolve a name at all (an untyped receiver), neither side is counted.

RepositoryFoundPrecisionWith -kind:attributeBefore October 2026
pallets/flask98.1%87.3%88.4% found, 93.8% precision57.4% found, 90.9% precision
psf/requests100%90.2%93.6%, 90.9%74.4%, 88.9%
pallets/click97.1%91.3%92.7%, 95.6%65.5%, 93.6%
encode/httpx99.5%80.5%77.9%, 90.1%59.0%, 86.6%

CI runs it on Flask and Click (.github/workflows/ref-oracle.yml) and fails if a change misses or adds more references than that.

pip install jedi==0.20.1
python3 src/check/oracle/py_refs.py bench-corpus/flask > flask.json
cargo run --release --example ref_oracle -- --oracle flask.json
cargo run --release --example ref_oracle -- --oracle flask.json --drop-kind attribute

The report groups misses and extras by the syntax around the name, which says which row of the Python table each one is.

Other languages

There is no oracle for the other languages yet. Each form above is checked with a small file per language. To give a sense of scale, a one-off count in October 2026 took every function, method and type defined in a public repository and counted how many of its uses in code fall on a reference. The count is rough: some of those uses are not references at all (a local variable that shares a type’s name, an import line).

LanguageRepositoryUses found as references
Gospf13/cobra90%
Rustfast_code_search src/53%
Javagoogle/gson52%
C#JamesNK/Newtonsoft.Json50%
TypeScriptcolinhacks/zod40%
TypeScriptsindresorhus/ky38%
CDaveGamble/cJSON0%
C++fmtlib/fmt0%

Closing these gaps is on the roadmap.

Diff impact

fcs impact, POST /api/impact and the MCP tool diff_impact take a unified diff and say, for each function, class, method, type or constant it changes, which files use it. File-level impact (every file that imports a changed file, as change_impact and the dependency explorer show it) lists thousands of files for a busy module. This lists the files that name the changed thing.

$ fcs impact                    # your uncommitted changes (git diff HEAD)
$ fcs impact --staged           # what you are about to commit
$ fcs impact main               # everything since main, to the working tree
$ git diff main... | fcs impact --diff -

modified pkg/util.py: 2 changed symbols

pkg/util.py:12  function parse_date  (modified, signature)
  3 files (2 reference, 1 code, 0 text only); searched importers that name it
  pkg/api/views.py  [reference]
    41: when = parse_date(request.GET["since"])
  pkg/settings.py  [code]
    3: from pkg.util import parse_date as parse
  tests/test_util.py  [reference, test]
    18: assert parse_date("2024-01-02") == date(2024, 1, 2)
    … 4 more lines

(Made-up example.) Piped, the output is path:line:col:text, one line per use, for vim -q and other quickfix readers; --json gives the full report. The exit status is 0 when some file uses the changed code, 1 when none does, 2 on error.

How it works

  1. The diff. Each file in the diff is matched to an indexed file (by the repository root when given, then by the path search results print, then by a unique path suffix). The indexed copy is one side of the change; the other side is rebuilt by applying the diff to it, forwards or backwards, whichever applies. So the same answer comes from git diff of a watched working tree (the index holds the new side) and from a diff whose old side is indexed. A file the index does not hold, or whose indexed copy matches neither side, is listed with a note and not analysed.
  2. Changed symbols. Both sides are parsed with tree-sitter (Python, JavaScript, TypeScript, Rust) and each changed line is put in the innermost definition that contains it, so a change inside a method is a change to the method, not to the class. A change to a definition’s signature lines is marked signature. Definitions present on one side only are added or removed. Lines outside every definition (imports, module-level code) are counted on the file and not traced. Other languages get the definitions fcs’s symbol extractor finds, with a change put in the nearest definition above it (and a note saying so).
  3. Who uses it. For a function, class, type or constant, fcs walks the import graph from the changed file along “imported by” edges, but only into files that mention the name (checked with the trigram index first, then the recorded references, then the text) and into package entry files (__init__.py, index.*, mod.rs, lib.rs, main.rs), which re-export. An import that renames it (import { parse as p }, from x import parse as p, use x::parse as p) adds the new name to the search. A constructor (__init__, constructor) is searched as its class.
  4. Members. A method or field is used through a value whose type fcs does not know, so it is searched by name among every file that imports its file, directly or not. Files that also name the class come first. Methods are listed only where fcs recorded a reference to the name.
  5. Tiers. Each use line is a reference (a recorded call, type, value or attribute reference), code (the name as a whole word in code, such as an import line or a JSX tag) or text (only in a string or a comment: a dotted path in a settings file is a real use, prose is not). A file’s tier is its best line’s. Files are ordered by tier, then (members) whether they name the class, then code before tests, then by how widely used the file is, then path.

Nothing is dropped: every file found is counted in the totals and in the tier counts. What is listed is capped (200 files per symbol and 3 lines per file by default; -n/--lines, max_files/max_lines, 0 for all), and a capped list says so. At most 500 changed symbols are searched per request; the rest are listed without users, with a note.

Measured accuracy

On Django at 79502d0, for the 248 most recent commits that change Python files (the last 400 commits of history), each commit’s diff (old side: the commit’s parent, new side: the indexed tree) was analysed and compared with the files where Jedi resolves a use to the changed definition (src/check/oracle/py_impact.py). Found is the share of Jedi’s files that fcs lists, right the share of listed files Jedi confirms. Files the diff itself changes are left out of both. CI repeats this on every change to the impact code (.github/workflows/impact-oracle.yml, examples/impact_oracle.rs).

SymbolsScoredFoundRight
Functions, classes, constants226100% (1,267 of 1,267 files)79.5%
Methods and fields131100% (212 of 212)13.0%

“Right” is a lower bound: Jedi misses dynamic uses that fcs’s text tier finds, such as dotted paths in settings and mock.patch("pkg.mod.name"). For members, fcs finds every file but also lists the files that call a method of the same name on something else; files naming the class come first so they are read first.

The evaluation before this was built (Django and Sentry, Python and TypeScript, against Jedi and the TypeScript compiler’s findReferences) found the same picture: functions and classes 100% found and 83–91% right with all three tiers; references alone miss two thirds of TypeScript uses (JSX tags, re-exports, type positions), which is why the code and text tiers are kept.

Speed

On Django (5,700 files), the analysis of one commit’s diff takes 49 ms median and 309 ms p95 (commits that change many methods at once are the slow ones). The walk is lazy: a file is read only when the trigram index says it may hold the name. Measured during the evaluation on 348,000 files (16 copies of Sentry), one changed symbol took 0.5 ms median and 3 ms p95; a synthetic hub function called from 200,000 of 400,000 files took 2 s, reading each caller once.

What it does not do

  • No types. A method is found by name, so every save() in the importers is listed for a change to one save() (class-named files first). Uses through getattr, dependency injection, or a method passed as a value are not references, so they are found only by the code tier (functions, classes) or not at all (methods).
  • One level. It lists the direct users of each changed symbol. It does not then follow the functions those users define to their own users.
  • Import graph languages only for the walk. Python, JavaScript, TypeScript and Rust have import edges. In other languages (Go, Java, C#, C, C++ and the rest) a symbol is searched by name across the whole index, with a note, so same-named things elsewhere are listed too. C and C++ record no references, so their uses are code or text only. Per-language coverage of references: Reference support; of import edges: Dependency graph support.
  • Language-called methods. A change to __eq__, __str__ or __iter__ is found only where it is named explicitly; the note says so.
  • Module-level changes (a changed import, a top-level statement) are counted per file, not traced.
  • The index must hold one side. With file watching on (the default), the index is the working tree, so fcs impact (uncommitted changes) and fcs impact REV (REV to the working tree) work. A diff between two other commits works only if the index holds one of them; otherwise each file says “matches neither side”.
  • gRPC has no impact call; use REST, MCP or fcs.
  • fcs impact is a subcommand, so searching for the word impact needs fcs search impact.

fcs command line

Full help for the client and each subcommand, as printed by the binary. The narrative guide is Command-line client.

fcs --help

Search the code index kept by a running fast_code_search server.

`fcs QUERY` is `fcs search QUERY`. The query is the web UI's: literal text, like rg -F (several words are one phrase; quotes and a leading - are text), plus file:PATTERN, -file:PATTERN, lang:rust, case:yes, word:yes. Put a query starting with - after --.

Exit status: 0 = matches found, 1 = no matches, 2 = error.

Usage: fcs [OPTIONS] <COMMAND>

Commands:
  search    Search file contents (the default; `fcs QUERY` works too)
  refs      Find references (call sites, type mentions) of an identifier; `kind:call`, `kind:type`, `kind:value`, `kind:attribute` keep one kind
  symbols   Search symbol definitions (functions, types, classes, ...) only
  impact    The files that use each function, class, method or constant a diff changes (default: your uncommitted changes, `git diff HEAD`)
  status    Show whether the server is reachable and what it has indexed
  mcp       Serve MCP over stdio by relaying to the server's /mcp endpoint (for agents that launch a command rather than connect to a URL)
  check     Check fcs against independent tools on your own code: generated queries against ripgrep, and the import graph against Python's and TypeScript's own resolvers when they are installed
  diagnose  Summarize the server's diagnostic captures (written when `[diagnostics] capture = true` and the server is slow or stuck)
  help      Print this message or the help of the given subcommand(s)

Options:
      --server <URL>
          Server base URL (default: $FCS_SERVER, then the config's web_address, then http://127.0.0.1:8080)

      --config <FILE>
          Configuration file (default: $FCS_CONFIG, ./fast_code_search.toml, ~/.config/fast_code_search/config.toml)

      --offline
          Search the on-disk index directly instead of asking the server

      --no-offline
          Never fall back to the on-disk index when the server is unreachable

      --index-path <FILE>
          Index file for offline searches (default: the config's index_path)

      --format <FORMAT>
          Output layout (default: grouped on a terminal, vimgrep when piped)

          Possible values:
          - grouped: File headings with `line:col: text` underneath (default on a terminal)
          - vimgrep: One `path:line:col:text` line per hit (default when piped)
          - json:    The server's JSON response, one object

      --json
          Shorthand for --format json

  -l, --files-with-matches
          Print every file that matches, once, best first (pages through all hits)

      --absolute
          Print absolute paths (needs the roots from the configuration)

      --color <COLOR>
          Colour output: auto (terminal only, honours NO_COLOR), always, never
          
          [default: auto]
          [possible values: auto, always, never]

  -q, --quiet
          No summary or fallback notes on stderr

  -h, --help
          Print help (see a summary with '-h')

  -V, --version
          Print version

Examples:
  fcs 'fn main'                 lines holding the phrase
  fcs -- -Wall                  a query starting with -
  fcs -e 'fn\s+\w+\(' -g '*.rs'  regex, Rust files only
  fcs refs SearchEngine         call sites and type mentions
  fcs symbols parse_query       definitions only
  fcs impact                    files that use what your uncommitted changes touch
  fcs -l TODO | xargs $EDITOR   files containing TODO
  vim -q <(fcs --format vimgrep TODO)
  fcs status                    is the server up, what does it hold
  fcs mcp                       MCP over stdio, for coding agents
  fcs check                     compare fcs with ripgrep and the import resolvers
  fcs diagnose                  what the server's diagnostic captures say

fcs search --help

Search file contents (the default; `fcs QUERY` works too)

Usage: fcs search [OPTIONS] <QUERY>...

Arguments:
  <QUERY>...
          The query (web UI syntax; several words are one phrase)

Options:
  -e, --regex
          Treat the query as a regular expression

  -s, --case-sensitive
          Match case exactly

  -i, --ignore-case
          Ignore case (the default for plain text; with -e, makes a regex ignore it too)

  -w, --word
          Match whole words only

  -n, --max <N>
          Maximum hits to print (1 to the server's search.max_page_size, 1000; default 50). With -l, the most files to list (default: every file)

      --offset <N>
          Skip the first N hits (paging; ordering is deterministic)
          
          [default: 0]

  -C, --context <N>
          Lines of context before and after each hit (0 to search.max_context_lines, 10)
          
          [default: 0]

  -g, --glob <GLOB>
          Only files matching this glob (repeatable; `src/**/*.rs`, `*.py`, `tests`)

      --exclude <GLOB>
          Skip files matching this glob (repeatable)

      --server <URL>
          Server base URL (default: $FCS_SERVER, then the config's web_address, then http://127.0.0.1:8080)

      --config <FILE>
          Configuration file (default: $FCS_CONFIG, ./fast_code_search.toml, ~/.config/fast_code_search/config.toml)

      --offline
          Search the on-disk index directly instead of asking the server

      --timeout-ms <MS>
          Stop scanning after this many milliseconds and return the best so far
          
          [default: 0]

      --max-files <N>
          Most candidate files to read, best first (default: the server's search.fast_rank_files, no limit)

      --no-offline
          Never fall back to the on-disk index when the server is unreachable

      --deps <PATH[:N]>
          Only search this file and what it imports, N hops deep (1 by default; `*` for any distance). Repeatable; `-PATH` leaves them out

      --index-path <FILE>
          Index file for offline searches (default: the config's index_path)

      --format <FORMAT>
          Output layout (default: grouped on a terminal, vimgrep when piped)

          Possible values:
          - grouped: File headings with `line:col: text` underneath (default on a terminal)
          - vimgrep: One `path:line:col:text` line per hit (default when piped)
          - json:    The server's JSON response, one object

      --rdeps <PATH[:N]>
          Only search this file and the files that import it (as `--deps`)

      --json
          Shorthand for --format json

  -l, --files-with-matches
          Print every file that matches, once, best first (pages through all hits)

      --absolute
          Print absolute paths (needs the roots from the configuration)

      --color <COLOR>
          Colour output: auto (terminal only, honours NO_COLOR), always, never
          
          [default: auto]
          [possible values: auto, always, never]

  -q, --quiet
          No summary or fallback notes on stderr

  -h, --help
          Print help (see a summary with '-h')

Usage: fcs impact [OPTIONS] [REV]...

          Revisions to diff, as `git diff` takes them: none for uncommitted changes (against HEAD), `REV` for REV to the working tree, `A..B` or `A B` between commits (the index must hold one of the two sides)

fcs check --help

Check fcs against independent tools on your own code: generated queries against ripgrep, and the import graph against Python's and TypeScript's own resolvers when they are installed

Usage: fcs check [OPTIONS] [PATH]...

Arguments:
  [PATH]...
          Folders to check (default: the configuration's indexed paths, else the current folder). They are indexed afresh, in memory

Options:
      --queries <N>
          Queries made up from the files and compared with `rg -F`
          
          [default: 200]

      --seed <N>
          Seed for the queries, to repeat a run (default: a fresh one)

      --rg <PATH>
          The ripgrep binary
          
          [default: rg]

      --no-search
          Skip the search check

      --no-graph
          Skip the dependency graph check

      --live
          Check the running server's index instead (see --server): does it agree with itself, does indexed content match the disk, and does it hold the files a fresh walk finds. Prints counts only, so the output is safe to share; add --show-paths for example file names

      --content <N>
          With --live: files whose content to read and check, spread over the index (0 skips it)
          
          [default: 2000]

      --all-content
          With --live: read and check every indexed file (slow on large trees)

      --no-discovery
          With --live: skip the walk of the disk

      --server <URL>
          Server base URL (default: $FCS_SERVER, then the config's web_address, then http://127.0.0.1:8080)

      --config <FILE>
          Configuration file (default: $FCS_CONFIG, ./fast_code_search.toml, ~/.config/fast_code_search/config.toml)

      --show-paths
          With --live: show example file names for each kind of problem

      --offline
          Search the on-disk index directly instead of asking the server

      --no-offline
          Never fall back to the on-disk index when the server is unreachable

      --index-path <FILE>
          Index file for offline searches (default: the config's index_path)

      --format <FORMAT>
          Output layout (default: grouped on a terminal, vimgrep when piped)

          Possible values:
          - grouped: File headings with `line:col: text` underneath (default on a terminal)
          - vimgrep: One `path:line:col:text` line per hit (default when piped)
          - json:    The server's JSON response, one object

      --json

          [default: auto]

Server command line

High-performance, in-memory code search service with trigram indexing and symbol awareness

Usage: fast_code_search_server [OPTIONS]

Options:
  -c, --config <FILE>       Path to configuration file
  -a, --address <ADDR>      gRPC listen address (overrides config file)
      --web-address <ADDR>  Web UI / REST listen address (overrides config file)
  -i, --index <PATH>        Additional paths to index (can be repeated, adds to config file paths)
      --no-auto-index       Skip automatic indexing on startup
      --no-grpc             Do not start the gRPC API (same as `enable_grpc = false` in the config)
  -v, --verbose             Enable verbose logging
      --init <FILE>         Generate a template configuration file and exit
      --static-dir <DIR>    Serve static UI files from this directory instead of the embedded assets. Useful during development: UI changes are visible without recompiling. Example: --static-dir static
  -h, --help                Print help
  -V, --version             Print version

Configuration template

What fast_code_search_server --init config.toml writes: every key with its default and the comment that explains it. The narrative reference is API and configuration.

# Fast Code Search Configuration
# Generated template - customize as needed

[server]
# Address to bind the gRPC server to.
# Loopback by default: the server has no authentication and serves full file
# contents. Use "0.0.0.0:50051" only on a trusted network.
address = "127.0.0.1:50051"

# Start the gRPC API (default: true). Set false if you only use the web UI,
# REST API and fcs CLI. If the gRPC port is busy at startup the server logs
# an error and keeps running without gRPC rather than exiting.
enable_grpc = true

# Address to bind the HTTP/Web UI server to (same caveat as above)
web_address = "127.0.0.1:8080"

# Enable the web UI and REST API (default: true)
enable_web_ui = true

# Origins allowed to call the REST API from another site (CORS).
# Empty (default) = same-origin only, which is all the embedded UI needs.
# Example: ["http://localhost:3000"], or ["*"] to allow any origin
cors_origins = []

# Searches executing at once on the REST API; extra requests get 503 + Retry-After
max_concurrent_searches = 64

# Per-request timeout (seconds) for both servers
request_timeout_secs = 30

# Serve static UI files from a directory on disk instead of embedded assets.
# When set, the web server reads HTML/CSS/JS files from this path on every
# request so that UI edits are visible without recompiling the server.
# Useful during development. Unset (the default) = the assets embedded in the
# binary; TOML has no "unset" value, so this one key stays commented out.
# static_dir = "static"

# Web UI theme for people who haven't picked one: "bold", "corporate" or
# "corporate-dark". Each browser remembers its own choice.
default_theme = "bold"

# Ask people to pick a theme on their first visit. Set false on a shared
# server to use default_theme without asking (they can still change it from
# the palette button in the header).
theme_picker = true

[search]
# Search limits for every front end (web UI, REST, gRPC, fcs, MCP). The
# defaults were measured on a 372k-file corpus; see the "Limits" page of the
# book. Every limit that cuts a search is reported with the result.

# A search reads candidate files best first (by a per-file score that needs
# no reads) until it has collected enough matches to rank. This caps how
# many files it reads unless the request asks for more. 0 = no cap.
fast_rank_files = 0

# Most files a request may ask for (`max_files` on /api/search, the web UI's
# SEARCH MORE FILES). 0 = no limit; set one on a shared server.
max_fast_rank_files = 0

# Matches collected to rank a page. Every page ending before result 1000
# collects multiplier x 1000, between min and max; every page from there on
# collects max. One budget per range keeps offset pages consistent. A page
# is the best of what was collected, so a small budget gives a poor page 1
# for common words. Each match costs about 230 bytes while the search runs.
min_match_budget = 32768
match_budget_multiplier = 8
max_match_budget = 100000

# Most hits one file contributes to pages before result 1000 (its best, by
# score). 1000 leaves out nothing those pages could show.
per_file_matches = 1000

# Page size when a request gives none, and the largest it may ask for (the
# web UI's LOAD MORE and MCP follow max_page_size).
default_page_size = 50
max_page_size = 1000

# Deepest result offset a client may page to. Deep pages cost a scan of that
# many matches. 0 = no limit.
max_page_offset = 100000

# Most path matches (the FILES box) one request may ask for.
max_path_matches = 100

# Longest deadline a request may ask for, in seconds (request_timeout_secs
# still bounds every search).
max_timeout_secs = 30

# Most context lines around each hit.
max_context_lines = 10

[mcp]
# Hits search_code returns by default, and the most one call may ask for
# (at most search.max_page_size; agents page further with `offset`).
default_limit = 50
max_limit = 1000

# Files whose path matches the query, listed above search_code's hits.
file_matches = 20

# Most context lines search_code may ask for.
max_context_lines = 5

# read_file: lines returned by default, and the most one call returns.
read_file_lines = 200
max_read_file_lines = 1000

# Graph tools: files listed per level or group by default, and at most.
graph_default_limit = 50
graph_max_limit = 500

[indexer]
# Paths to index on startup
# Add your project directories here
paths = [
    # "C:/code/my-project",
    # "C:/code/another-project",
    # "/home/user/projects/my-app",
]

# Honour .gitignore / .ignore files under the indexed paths (default: true)
respect_gitignore = true

# File extensions to include (empty = all text files)
# Example: ["rs", "py", "js", "ts", "go", "c", "cpp", "h", "java"]
include_extensions = []

# More extensions never to read, on top of the built-in binary list
# (images, archives, fonts, office documents, media, compiled objects)
# Example: ["dat", "parquet"]
extra_binary_extensions = []

# Patterns to exclude from indexing
exclude_patterns = [
    "**/node_modules/**",
    "**/target/**",
    "**/.git/**",
    "**/build/**",
    "**/dist/**",
    "**/__pycache__/**",
    "**/venv/**",
    "**/.venv/**",
]

# Exact file paths to permanently exclude from indexing.
# Useful for files that cause crashes or are too large/noisy to be useful.
# After a crash, check fcs_last_processed.txt to identify the offending file.
# Example: ["/repo/src/generated/huge_file.rs"]
exclude_files = []

# Maximum file size to index in bytes (default: 10MB)
max_file_size = 10485760

# Enable encoding detection for non-UTF-8 text files (default: true)
# When enabled, files in Latin-1, Shift-JIS, UTF-16 etc. are transcoded to UTF-8.
# Disable for UTF-8-only codebases for slightly faster indexing.
transcode_non_utf8 = true

# Path to persistent index storage.
# The index is saved here and loaded on restart, so a restart takes seconds
# and only changed files are re-read. Remove the line to keep the index in
# memory only: it is then rebuilt from scratch on every start, and the server
# logs a warning saying so. Use one file per config. The directory is created
# on first save; `~` is expanded and relative paths resolve against this file.
index_path = "~/.local/share/fast_code_search/config.fcsidx"

# Save index after initial build completes (default: true)
# Only effective when index_path is set
save_after_build = true

# Save index after N file updates via watcher (default: 0 = disabled)
# When enabled, the index is periodically saved after this many watcher-triggered updates.
# Useful for long-running servers to persist incremental changes.
save_after_updates = 0

# Checkpoint every N files during the initial index build (default: 0 = disabled)
# If the process is killed mid-build, the next run resumes from the checkpoint.
# Recommended for very large repos: 20000. Has no effect if index_path is not set.
checkpoint_interval_files = 0

# Enable file watcher for incremental indexing (default: true)
# Changes to indexed files are detected and re-indexed automatically.
# Set to false to index once at startup (search then misses later edits
# until a restart; responses say how old the index is).
watch = true

# Enable tree-sitter symbol extraction (default: true)
# When enabled, function/class/struct definitions are parsed from source files
# and used to boost search relevance for symbol matches.
# Disable to reduce memory usage and indexing time at the cost of reduced relevance.
enable_symbols = true

# Start result paths with the indexed folder's name (default: true).
# Indexing /code/myapp shows myapp/src/main.rs; false shows src/main.rs.
# Only applies when a single path is indexed. Display only: file: filters
# and the saved index are unaffected.
show_root_name = true

# Files read and indexed per batch during the initial build (default: 500).
# Peak RAM scales with batch_size x average file size x ~4: lower it on small
# machines, raise it on large ones to reduce lock contention.
batch_size = 500

[telemetry]
# Enable OpenTelemetry trace export (default: false)
# Set to true to enable OTLP export (console logging is always active)
# Env overrides: OTEL_SDK_DISABLED=true, FCS_TRACING_ENABLED=true
enabled = false

# OTLP gRPC exporter endpoint (default: http://localhost:4317)
# Env override: OTEL_EXPORTER_OTLP_ENDPOINT
otlp_endpoint = "http://localhost:4317"

# Service name reported to the collector
# Env override: OTEL_SERVICE_NAME
service_name = "fast_code_search"

[diagnostics]
# Self-diagnosis: when the server is slow or stuck, write what it was doing
# to a JSON file you can look at later with `fcs diagnose`. Nothing leaves
# the machine. Off by default.
capture = false

# Where captures go. Empty: a `diagnostics` folder next to index_path.
dir = ""

# What counts as slow or stuck, in seconds. The defaults sit well above what
# was measured on a healthy server under heavy load (see the book's
# "Diagnostic captures" page).
slow_search_secs = 5.0
lock_wait_secs = 2.0
stall_secs = 120.0
backlog_secs = 300.0

# Seconds before the same kind of capture is written again, and how many
# captures (and megabytes) are kept; the oldest are deleted.
min_interval_secs = 300.0
max_files = 50
max_total_mb = 100

# Log lines kept in each capture.
log_lines = 300

# On a hang, record every thread's stack with gdb or eu-stack if installed
# (pauses the server for a second or two).
thread_stacks = true
