# Keep query text, file paths and quoted text in captures. Off: captures say
# how long a query was, not what it was.
include_queries = false

# Background scrub: check indexed files against the disk a few at a time,
# and index again any the index has out of step (a lost change). Counts are
# in /api/diagnostics and `fcs check --live`. About 0.1% of a core.
scrub = true
scrub_files_per_minute = 3000
# Minutes between checks of the index's own consistency (0: never).
scrub_invariants_minutes = 60.0

Index file format

The persisted index (index_path) is a single file, format v12, magic FCSIDX09. Everything is little-endian.

magic[8]  version:u32  crc:u32  meta_len:u64  dir_count:u32  bitmaps_len:u64   (36-byte header)
META     bincode-encoded metadata (see below)
DIR      dir_count × { trigram[3], pad[1], offset:u64, len:u32 }, sorted by trigram
BITMAPS  the Roaring bitmaps, back to back; DIR offsets index this region

crc is a CRC-32 over META, DIR and BITMAPS. On load the file is memory-mapped, the magic and both version fields are checked, the section lengths are validated against the file size, the checksum is verified, and the directory is checked to be sorted and in bounds before any bincode decoding happens, so a truncated, corrupt or foreign file is rejected early and cheaply. The server then keeps the file mapped and reads the bitmaps in place: a query decodes only the lists for its trigrams, and the lists stay in the page cache instead of on the heap (on Windows, which cannot replace a mapped file, they are decoded at load).

Metadata section

  • the configuration fingerprint (with the extraction schema tag, see Upgrading) and the indexed root paths;
  • the file table: path (raw bytes, so non-UTF-8 names survive), mtime in nanoseconds, size, and which configured root the file belongs to;
  • per-file symbols and references (reference names interned in one table);
  • resolved dependency edges and imports still waiting for their target;
  • the symbol name dictionary symbol search filters by (see Candidates): the distinct case-folded names, the files and symbol kinds behind each, and the files it does not describe yet. Saved so a load need not rebuild it (about 2 s at 302,000 files).
  • folder stamps from the last complete walk: each folder’s modification time, a fingerprint of its ignore files and how many eligible files it held (v12), and a fingerprint of the user’s global excludes file, so a restart re-reads only the folders that changed (see Persistence).

A load gives each file its position as its id, so every section is used as saved; files changed or deleted since the save are tombstoned rather than renumbered. Two kinds of save fill the table differently:

  • a checkpoint, while the server runs, keeps every id: a file removed since the load is an empty slot (empty path), and the metadata lists the slots (dead_slots). Nothing is renumbered, a posting list no change touched is copied as its bytes, and the bitmaps may still name the empty slots, which a load masks out.
  • a full save, at shutdown, compacts: removed files are absent and every other section is remapped to the new positions.

Guarantees

  • Atomic. A save writes a unique temporary file next to the target, fsyncs it, and renames it into place; saves are serialized within the process. A crash leaves the previous index intact, and the next start removes the temporary file a killed save left.
  • Detects drift. Each file’s mtime and size at read time are stored; on load a file whose stat differs is re-read.
  • Pinned. tests/fixtures/index-v12.fcsidx is compared byte for byte in the test suite; a deliberate format change bumps the magic and regenerates the fixture (FCS_WRITE_GOLDEN=1 cargo test golden).
  • Older formats are rejected with a message asking for a rebuild; they are never partially decoded.

The implementation is src/index/persistence.rs.

Indexing pipeline

A build turns a set of directories into three structures held in memory: a trigram index, per-file symbol and reference tables, and an import graph. Searches are served throughout.

flowchart TB
    R[(roots)] --> D[Discovery<br/>excludes, extensions,<br/>size, .gitignore]
    D --> B{{batch of files}}
    B --> T[Read + trigrams<br/>parallel]
    B --> S[Symbols, references,<br/>imports<br/>tree-sitter, parallel]
    T --> M[Merge<br/>under the write lock]
    S --> M
    M --> I[(trigram index)]
    M --> Y[(symbol + reference tables)]
    M --> G[(import graph)]
    M -. every N files .-> P[/checkpoint to index_path/]
    I --> Q[searches served<br/>between batches]
    Y --> Q
    G --> Q
  1. Discovery. Each root is walked with the exclude patterns, the include-extension list, the binary and size rules and .gitignore applied. Excluded directories are never entered. The same rule set (one EligibilityProbe) decides for watcher events and for reconciliation on load, so the three can never disagree.
  2. Read and trigrams, in parallel. Every file is read into an owned buffer (never through a live memory map, so a file being rewritten cannot crash the process), transcoded if it is not UTF-8, and reduced to its set of lowercase trigrams. The mtime and size at read time are recorded for later staleness checks.
  3. Symbols, in parallel. tree-sitter parses the file with the grammar’s tags.scm query (plus supplements: Rust scoped and generic calls and type mentions, TypeScript call sites) under a timeout and a panic guard. Definitions become symbols; call sites and type mentions become references; import statements are collected.
  4. Merge, under the write lock. Files are registered, postings inserted (in parallel: the posting lists are split into 64 shards by trigram, and each shard takes its share of the whole batch on its own thread), symbols and references stored, imports resolved against the files known so far; unresolved imports are parked and retried when a file with a matching name appears. Batches are small so a search only ever waits for one merge.
  5. Checkpoint and save. Every checkpoint_interval_files files and at the end, the index is written atomically to index_path (Persistence).

Per-file metadata for fast ranking (symbol density, dependents, location, test-path penalty) is computed after the build and refreshed as files change.

Languages

Symbol-aware indexing covers Rust, Python, JavaScript, TypeScript, Go, C, C++, Java, C#, Ruby, PHP and Bash; JSON, TOML, YAML, HTML, CSS and Markdown get structural symbols (keys, headings). Every other text file is indexed for full-text and regex search without symbols.

Candidates and regex analysis

A search never scans the whole corpus. It first computes a candidate set from the trigram index, then reads only those files.

Plain text

The query is case-folded (Unicode simple case folding, the fold ripgrep’s -i uses, which is lowercase except for a few letters such as ſ and µ; the index stores folded text) and split into overlapping three-byte windows; parse_query yields par, ars, rse, se_, e_q, _qu, que, uer, ery. Each trigram has a posting list (a Roaring bitmap of file ids); intersecting them gives every file that could contain the query, independent of corpus size. Several words are one phrase, so their trigrams (spaces included) are intersected the same way.

Queries shorter than three characters have no trigrams and fall back to all files, bounded by the match budget and ranking mode.

Regex

The pattern is parsed into a syntax tree and walked for required literals: text that every match must contain. Alternations contribute the union of their branches’ literals (each branch must have one), optional groups contribute nothing, and a repetition ends a literal run (so fo+bar requires fo and obar, never fobar). Case-insensitive patterns lower every literal, with (?i) handled through simple case folding so k and s do not break a run. The literals’ trigrams filter the candidates exactly as a text query would; a pattern with no required literal of three or more bytes scans everything.

A property test in the suite asserts that for every pattern the candidate set is a superset of the files the compiled regex actually matches.

Verification and modes

Candidates are then read and verified: the text query is searched with a whole-buffer scan (memchr-based, or a Unicode case fold when needed) that resolves line boundaries only at hits; a regex is compiled once per query with (?mR) and run in one pass per file with one hit per line. Symbol queries match definition names through a dictionary of every distinct case-folded name (one string, searched with memmem) that lists the files defining each name and the kinds of symbol it names there. A case-insensitive query ranks files from the matching names alone; a case-sensitive one tests the symbols of those files only. Files whose symbols changed after the dictionary was built are tested symbol by symbol, and the dictionary is rebuilt once a sixteenth of the files (at least 4,096) have changed. Reference queries look up the interned identifier in each candidate’s reference table and verify the position against the current content.

Budgets

Every search runs under a QueryRun: a match budget derived from the page size and offset ([search] min_match_budget, match_budget_multiplier, max_match_budget), and a deadline just under the request timeout. When either stops the scan the response says so (truncated_by_budget). A deep page gets a budget that reaches it, so paging past search.max_page_offset (100,000 by default) is refused rather than allowed to grow the budget without bound.

How the import graph is built

This page explains how fcs turns Python and JavaScript/TypeScript imports into edges between files, and how it decides that an import is internal (an edge to a file in your project) or external (a package, which gets no edge). Dependency graph support is the short reference: a table of which import forms are handled. This page is the method behind that table.

The rule that decides internal vs external

fcs has no list of package names, and it does not read requirements.txt, pyproject.toml, package.json dependencies or a lockfile. (For JavaScript and TypeScript it reads tsconfig.json and each indexed package.json’s own name and entry points, described below.) The decision is made by one rule, the same for every language:

An import is internal when the language’s own lookup rules, applied to the files fcs has indexed, find a file. Otherwise it is unresolved, and it gets no edge.

So “internal” means “resolves to an indexed file”, and everything else falls into one of two kinds of unresolved import:

KindHow fcs tellsExamplesWhat it means
ExternalWritten as a package name (not as a path into the project), and no indexed file matchesimport os, import numpy, from django.db import models when Django is not indexed, import React from 'react', lodash/merge, @scope/pkgThe standard library or an installed package. This is expected and is not a problem.
Local but unresolvedWritten as a path into the project (.x, ./x, ../x, @/x, ~/x) and no indexed file matchesfrom .missing import x, import './old-name'A broken import, a file that is excluded from the index, or a form fcs does not handle yet (see the support table)

The test for “written as a path into the project” is purely about the text: it starts with ., @/, ~/, or Rust’s crate::, self::, super::. import pkg.missing written as an absolute Python name cannot be told apart from a package, so it counts as external.

Two consequences follow from the rule.

  • What is indexed decides what is internal. Third-party code is not indexed by default: node_modules, venv, .venv, build and dist are excluded. So installed packages are external. If you index a dependency’s source tree on purpose (for example, Django next to your project), imports of it become internal edges, as long as the lookup rules below reach it.
  • fcs never guesses by name. If import utils cannot be found by the lookup rules, fcs does not settle for “some file called utils.py somewhere in the repository”. A missing edge is better than a wrong one, because a wrong edge silently corrupts “imported by N”, ranking and the dependency explorer.

The pipeline

Both languages go through the same three steps.

  1. Extract. Each file is parsed with its tree-sitter grammar, in the same pass that extracts symbols. Every import statement becomes an import string (the module text as written) and the line it is on. Nothing is executed and no interpreter or compiler is run.
  2. Resolve. The import string is turned into candidate file paths using the language’s rules (below). A candidate counts only if it is an indexed file. The check is a lookup in the index’s path table; the disk is not consulted. The first candidate that is indexed becomes the edge.
  3. Keep the rest. An import that resolves to nothing is kept, together with its file and line. When a file is indexed later (a new file, a rename, a watcher event), the imports that mention its name are tried again. So an edge appears as soon as its target exists, and order of indexing does not matter.

Python

What is extracted

SourceImport string(s)
import a.ba.b
import a, b as ca, b (one per module; the alias is ignored)
from a.b import c, da.b.c, a.b.d (one per name)
from a import *a
from . import x.x
from ..pkg import x..pkg.x

from a.b import c gives a.b.c because c may be a submodule (a/b/c.py) or just a name defined in a/b.py. Resolution tries the submodule first and falls back to the module, which is what Python does. Imports inside functions, if TYPE_CHECKING: blocks and try: blocks are extracted like any other. importlib.import_module("x") and __import__ are not, because their argument is a runtime value.

Relative imports (from .x import y)

These are always internal or local-but-unresolved, never external.

  1. The dots choose the base folder. One dot is the importing file’s own folder (its package), and each extra dot goes one folder up.
  2. Under that base, fcs looks for the dotted name as a module or package, longest first. For .a.b it tries a/b.py, then a/b/__init__.py, then a.py, then a/__init__.py.
  3. A match must cover the whole name, or all of it but the last part (the last part may be a name defined in the module). Anything shorter is not accepted, because Python would fail that import too.
  4. from . import name, where name is not a module, resolves to the package’s own __init__.py.
pkg/__init__.py
pkg/api.py           from .models import User   -> pkg/models.py
pkg/models.py        from . import api          -> pkg/api.py
pkg/sub/views.py     from ..models import User  -> pkg/models.py
                     from .missing import x     -> unresolved (local)

Absolute imports (import a.b, from a import b)

Python 3 looks for an absolute import only on sys.path, never next to the importing file. fcs does not know your sys.path, so it works out the likely entries from the folder layout.

  1. Find the roots. Walk up from the importing file’s folder, all the way up, however deep the file sits. A folder that contains __init__.py is part of a package, so it is not a root. Every other folder is a candidate root, and so is its src/ subfolder (the “src layout”).
  2. Look under each root for the dotted name, longest first, exactly as for relative imports: a/b.py, a/b/__init__.py, a.py, a/__init__.py. The same “whole name, or all but the last part” rule applies.
  3. Pick the best root. A root where the whole name exists wins immediately. Otherwise the root that matches the most of the name wins, and the nearest root wins a tie.
  4. A repository root that is itself a package. When no root found anything and the outermost package folder (the top of a chain of __init__.py folders) is the repository root, or no folder above it holds an indexed file, that folder is tried as a root too. This is the repository with an __init__.py in its root folder that is run with that folder on sys.path: import app.models from repo/app/views.py finds repo/app/models.py. Only the outermost package qualifies, so import json inside repo/app/fields/ is still the standard library.
  5. Nothing found means external. import os, import requests and from django.db import models find nothing in the index (the standard library and site-packages are not under any of these roots), so they resolve to nothing.
myproject/                  (no __init__.py: a root)
  src/shop/__init__.py
  src/shop/cart.py          from shop.models import Item  -> src/shop/models.py
  src/shop/models.py        import json                   -> external (stdlib)
  src/shop/json.py
  tests/test_cart.py        from shop.cart import Cart    -> src/shop/cart.py

import json in src/shop/models.py does not resolve to the src/shop/json.py next to it. src/shop/ is a package, so it is not on sys.path, and Python would load the standard library’s json. A tests/test_cart.py that imports shop finds it through the myproject/src/ root.

Python limits

  • Only the folder layout is used. sys.path entries from pyproject.toml, setup.cfg, PYTHONPATH, .pth files or editable installs are not read, so a package that lives somewhere these rules do not reach is external to fcs.
  • Namespace packages (folders without __init__.py) work when they sit under a root, because each segment is a folder name.
  • .pyi stub files are indexed and their imports are extracted, but they are never the target of an import.
  • A name that exists both in your project and in the standard library (say a top-level logging.py in a root) resolves to your file, as it would for Python with that root first on sys.path.

JavaScript and TypeScript

What is extracted

SourceImport string
import x from './a', import './a', import type { T } from './a'./a
export { x } from './a', export * from './a'./a
require('./a'), require(`./a`)./a
import('./a'), import(/* chunk */ './a')./a
import data from './a.json' with { type: 'json' } (or assert)./a.json

A template string with ${…}, or any argument that is not a string literal, is a runtime value and is skipped. TypeScript’s older import x = require('./a') is extracted as ./a.

How a specifier is classified

The specifier’s first characters decide which lookups apply, in this order:

SpecifierLookupIf nothing is found
./x, ../x, ., ..Relative to the importing file’s folderLocal but unresolved
anything else1. The nearest tsconfig.json (or jsconfig.json): its paths (an exact pattern, else the wildcard pattern with the longest prefix, each target in order), then its baseUrl, with relative extends applied, as tsc does(next step)
2. A workspace package: an indexed package.json whose name is the package part (@acme/shared of @acme/shared/money). The bare name goes to its entry point (types, typings, source, module, main, then exports["."]), then src/index, then index; a subpath goes to src/money, then money(next step)
3. @/x, ~/x: from the nearest parent folder with a package.json or tsconfig.json (else the repository root): src/x first, then xLocal but unresolved
4. When no tsconfig sets baseUrl or paths: the repository root (the folder holding .git) as baseUrl, so components/Button finds <repo>/components/Button.tsxExternal

Each step only counts when it finds an indexed file, so react and lodash/merge stay external: no tsconfig alias, no indexed package.json and no file under the repository root carries that name. package.json imports (#name) are not read yet and show up as missing edges.

tsconfig, package.json and .git are read from disk (once per folder, and again when a tsconfig*.json or package.json is re-indexed), so a tsconfig excluded from the index still applies. The graph is built when files are indexed: after changing paths or a package’s name, re-index.

Finding the file

Given the folder path a specifier points at, fcs tries, in order:

  1. The exact file, when the specifier has an extension.
  2. The TypeScript extension swap. In TypeScript ESM code you write the extension of the compiled output, so ./a.js can mean a.ts or a.tsx, ./a.jsx can mean a.tsx or a.ts, ./a.mjs means a.mts and ./a.cjs means a.cts.
  3. Appended extensions: .ts .tsx .js .jsx .mjs .cjs .mts .cts .d.ts .json, in that order.
  4. A folder’s index file: index with each of the extensions in 3.
web/
  package.json
  src/app.tsx         import { api } from './api'      -> src/api.ts
                      import Button from '@/ui/Button'  -> src/ui/Button/index.tsx
                      import { money } from '@acme/shared' -> packages/shared/src/index.ts
                      import './styles.css'             -> src/styles.css (if indexed)
                      import React from 'react'         -> external
  src/api.ts          export * from './types.js'        -> src/types.ts

Imports of stylesheets, .vue or .svelte components and other assets resolve when that file is indexed, because a bundler resolves them and they are real dependencies. TypeScript itself does not resolve them.

JavaScript and TypeScript limits

  • package.json imports (#name), exports subpath maps and nested exports conditions are not read; rootDirs is not applied.
  • A tsconfig extends that names an npm package (@tsconfig/node20) is skipped; relative extends (a string or an array) are followed.
  • Aliases defined only in a bundler config (webpack, Vite, Jest) are not read. @/ and ~/ follow the common convention; other bundler-only aliases show up as missing edges.

Seeing what did not resolve

  • The dependency explorer’s folder map says how many imports written as project paths resolve to no file.
  • GET /api/graph/modules returns unresolved_imports (all of them, packages included) and unresolved_local (the ones written as project paths).
  • GET /api/graph/imports?path=… lists every import of a file with its line; unresolved ones have target: null.
  • MCP’s dependency_overview gives both counts, and file_dependencies lists a file’s unresolved imports.

A large unresolved_local count usually means a form from the limits above (a bundler-only alias, for example) or files excluded from the index.

How the method is checked

Rules like these are easy to get subtly wrong, so fcs compares its graph with each language’s own resolver, an oracle that knows nothing about fcs.

LanguageOracleHow it runs
Pythongrimp, the import-graph library behind import-linterEvery package directly under the folder (or its src/) is given to grimp with that folder on sys.path. grimp parses imports statically and resolves them as Python would. External packages are left out.
JavaScript, TypeScriptTypeScript’s own ts.resolveModuleNameEvery JS/TS file outside node_modules, dist and similar folders is pre-processed with ts.preProcessFile, and each import is resolved under the nearest tsconfig.json or jsconfig.json, with extends, paths and baseUrl applied (Bundler resolution when there is no config). A target in node_modules or outside the folder counts as external.

The comparison (src/check/graph.rs) works on edges between files:

  • Only files the oracle analysed are compared.
  • An edge the oracle draws and fcs does not is missing. An edge fcs draws and the oracle does not is extra, which means a wrong edge.
  • Missing edges are grouped by the shape of the import (relative, from pkg import name, @/ alias, bare name…), so each group points at one row of the support table.
  • Edges from TypeScript files to stylesheets, components and assets are counted separately and not judged, since TypeScript cannot resolve them.
  • Package-style imports that fcs links and the oracle leaves unresolved are listed separately and not counted as extra. The oracle runs without an install, so it cannot follow a workspace package’s node_modules link; fcs links it to the package’s source. The repository-root fallback lands here too.

CI runs both oracles on pinned public repositories and fails if a change adds a missing or extra edge:

LanguageRepositoryResult
PythonDjango 5.2 (grimp 3.17)3,041 of 3,041 edges, none extra
TypeScriptVite 6.3.5 (TypeScript 5.9)7 missing (4 package.json imports, 3 relative imports TypeScript resolves through a folder’s package.json or, where a.js and a.ts both exist, to a.ts), 1 extra; 221 workspace-package edges listed apart

To run the same comparison on your own code, run fcs check (see Correctness against ripgrep). It uses grimp if pip install grimp has been done, and TypeScript if it is in the repository’s node_modules. It reports missing and extra edges with examples, but does not fail on them: they are either a known gap from the limits above or a bug worth reporting.

Ranking

This is the part that makes results feel right. A hit’s score combines a content match with what the index knows about the line, the file and the codebase. All weights live in one file, src/search/ranking.rs.

flowchart LR
    subgraph line["the line"]
        direction TB
        A[defines a symbol ×3]
        B[exact case ×2]
        C[starts the line ×1.5]
        W[whole word ×1.5]
    end
    subgraph file["the file"]
        direction TB
        E[under src/ or lib/ ×1.5]
        F[test / example path ×0.6]
        H[public definition ×1.6]
    end
    subgraph codebase["the codebase"]
        G["imported by N files<br/>× (1 + 0.5·log10 N)"]
    end
    line --> S((score))
    file --> S
    codebase --> S

Line-level signals

score = base
      × 3.0   if the line defines a symbol
      × 2.0   if the match has the exact case typed
      × 1.5   if the match starts the (trimmed) line
      × 1.5   if the match is a whole word (text search)
      × 1.5   if the file is under src/ or lib/
      × (1 + 0.5 · log10(files that import this file))
      × max(1 / (1 + ln(1 + length/100)), 0.3)      # long lines damped

A plain search matches like rg -F, so class ModelForm also matches class ModelFormOptions:; the whole-word factor puts class ModelForm( first, although its line is longer.

The dependency factor is the import graph at work: a module that twenty files import scores 1.65× a module nothing imports, for the same line. Files that are imported are the ones people are looking for. The graph has import edges for Rust, Python and JavaScript/TypeScript only (see Dependency graph support). In a repository that mixes those with Go, Java, C#, C/C++ or Ruby, files in the other languages never get this factor, so a Go module twenty files import scores like a Rust module nothing imports.

Path signals read the path under its indexed root: src/ and lib/ are folders inside the repository, and the folders the repository sits in (~/src/…, /mnt/tests/…) never change a score. A test or example path is a folder whose name starts with test, example, mock or fixture, a __tests__ or spec folder, or a test file name: test_x.py, x_test.go, x_tests.rs, x.test.ts, x.spec.js, tests.rs. The factor applies to text, regex and symbol searches alike.

Definitions come from the symbols captured when the file was indexed. If the file has changed on disk since (file watching off, or not caught up), a line counts as a definition only while the recorded symbol’s name is still on it, so an edit above a definition drops its boost rather than moving it onto an unrelated line.

Result order

Results are in rank order with each file’s hits kept together: files in the order of their best hit, and every hit of a file, by score, before the next file. No hit waits behind other files’ hits; a file with a hundred matches takes as much of the page as it needs, as in ripgrep. Ties break on score, then file, then line, so paging is deterministic.

(From v0.12.0 to v0.15.2 results were interleaved by file instead: every file’s best hit, then every file’s second, so a file’s later hits could be pages away. That was never asked for and was removed.)

Every result carries file_hits, how many hits the search found in its file across all pages (file_hits_capped when the file reached the per-file cap and may hold more), so a file cut at a page boundary says “3 of 7 hits” in the web UI rather than “3 hits”.

Reading order

Every search reads its candidate files best first, by a per-file score that needs no I/O: base, src//lib/, code extension, log2(symbol count) (capped), log2(dependents) (capped), ×0.7 for test and example paths, ×5 when the query matches the file’s name. It stops when it has collected enough matches to rank the page (the match budget, see Search limits), so for a word with a million hits page 1 is the best of the best-looking files, not of the first folders on disk. The order is settled only as far as the search reads (a partial selection, then a sort of that part), so a search that stops a thousand files in does not sort the other 300,000. rank=auto and rank=fast also stop after search.fast_rank_files files when the server sets one (no limit by default; a request may ask for more with max_files, up to search.max_fast_rank_files); rank=full never stops at a file limit.

(Until 0.17, rank=auto read files in discovery order up to 5,000 candidates and only the best 2,000 above that, and the budget was 512 matches. On a 372k-file corpus that put 10% of the exhaustive top 50 on page 1 for common words; the current defaults put 81% there.)

Why there is no unranked mode

Ranking itself is a sort and costs little; the time goes into reading the matching files. A mode that stopped as soon as page 1 was full (as rg | head does) would skip most of that reading, but it would show the first matches found rather than the best. Measured with examples/rank_cost.rs on tokio 1.45.0 + Django 5.2 (7,580 files, warm cache, 4 vCPU, page of 50):

QueryMatchesRanked page 1Stop at 50 matchesBest 50 on that page
return22,38520.2 ms0.9 ms0 of 50
import14,94016.9 ms0.7 ms0 of 50
def test_16,52115.1 ms2.1 ms10 of 50
Config3,1997.6 ms0.2 ms2 of 50
unwrap()2,5223.3 ms0.3 ms36 of 50
TODO2642.2 ms0.9 ms44 of 50

The ranked page held the exhaustive top 50 for every query. On a tree ten times larger (75,800 files, ten copies of the same two) ranked page 1 took 17–80 ms and stopping at 50 matches 0.5–9 ms. A few tens of milliseconds is not worth a page that, for common words, holds none of the best hits, so every search is ranked. The rank parameter of the API, gRPC and fcs --rank is kept for old scripts; it only decides whether a file limit applies (see Reading order). Not measured yet: a cold cache or a slow disk, where reading costs more.

Symbols and references

Symbol search scores exact name matches above prefix matches above substrings, with variables and constants slightly below types and functions of the same name. Reference search returns call sites and type mentions of an identifier, verified against the current file content, and ranks them with the same file-level signals. What counts as a reference differs by language; see Reference support.

Worked examples

QueryFirst resultWhy
fn mainfn main() {a definition; start of line
SearchEnginepub struct SearchEngine {definition (3×) in src/ (1.5×) with many importers
impl SearchEngineimpl SearchEngine {the phrase at the start of the line, in src/
fcs refs prune_vanished_inthe call in incremental.rsthe reference table, not text matching
Widget in a tree with a widget_test.rsthe definition, then the importers, then the testtest paths are demoted

Measured ranking quality (precision@1 over a labelled query set) is part of the benchmark plan; until it lands, the examples above are covered by unit tests in src/search/engine/tests.rs.

Incremental updates

With watch = true the server follows the tree and keeps the index current without a rebuild.

Watching

On Linux one inotify watch is installed per directory that survives the exclude patterns, so node_modules, target and .git/objects cost no watches at all; new directories get watches when they appear. macOS (FSEvents) and Windows watch each root recursively.

Events are treated as hints, not facts. An event only says which paths may have changed; when the batch is applied, each path is checked against the disk: a file is indexed or dropped, and a directory is reconciled (new or changed files indexed, files that are gone removed). Backends merge, reorder and lose events, so nothing depends on the kind of an event or on matching the two halves of a rename. Events are gathered until there has been a quiet gap of 200 ms (at most two seconds), then applied as one batch.

When events are known to be lost (the OS reports an overflow, or the bounded queue fills up), the watched roots are reconciled with the disk instead. Reconciling skips files whose size and modification time match what was indexed, so it costs a walk and a stat per file, not a rebuild. A file that was read within 3 seconds of being modified is read again anyway: on file systems with coarse timestamps (FAT, HFS+, some network shares) it could have been rewritten in the same tick with the same size. A folder that the ignore files above it ignore (sub/ in the root .gitignore) is left out when it is reconciled, as the initial walk leaves it out.

Ignore files. A change to a .gitignore, .ignore or .rgignore (edited, created, deleted, or swapped by a branch switch) reconciles the folder it is in, so files it now ignores leave the index and files it no longer ignores join it, as a restart would. Before, each file kept its old state until the file itself changed.

Changes during the initial build

Files are read outside the write lock, so a file can be edited, renamed or deleted between being read by the initial build and being merged into the index. Before a batch is merged each file is checked again: content that is no longer on disk is never installed, a deleted file is skipped, and a rewritten one is read again.

Applying a batch

A batch is applied in three steps, and only the last one takes the write lock, so searches keep being answered while a batch is read from disk:

  1. Plan (under a read lock, which searches share). Events are coalesced per path. Deleted paths and directories are looked up; modified paths are checked against the disk (directories reconciled as above; vanished or newly ineligible paths dropped). As a safety net for backends that lose the delete half of a rename, the parent directory of every changed path is listed and entries whose file is gone are dropped. The index keeps its files by directory, so all of this costs the size of the batch and the directories it touches, not the size of the index.
  2. Read and parse (no lock). The files to (re)index are read and parsed in parallel on the watcher’s own threads (half the cores), so the searches running on the shared thread pool are not queued behind a burst.
  3. Install (write lock, in steps). The removals go first: one bit each for the posting lists read from the mapped index file (see Persistence), and a single pass over the lists held on the heap (all of them before the index is first saved); then the parsed files, 512 at a time, each step under its own write lock with a short pause between steps for the searches that arrived meanwhile. A step that had to wait for searches to get the lock keeps installing for about as long as it waited, up to one second (see “Catching up” below). A modified file keeps its id; edges into it are kept, and a file that was deleted and comes back regains its dependents. A path is looked up again at install time, since the index may have changed since it was planned (the initial build can run alongside), and a file that changed again since it was read has an event of its own on the way.

Symbol names. Symbol search finds files through a dictionary of symbol names built after a build and saved with the index (see Candidates). Files installed after that are searched symbol by symbol, which costs more per file, until the dictionary is rebuilt: once a sixteenth of the files (at least 4,096) have changed, inside the install step that crosses that line. Known limitation: that step holds the write lock for the rebuild too, about 2 s at 302,000 files, so searches arriving then wait that long, once per that many changed files.

Imports. Every file’s imports are kept as written. After each step the files whose imports could resolve differently now are resolved again: those that imported a removed file, and those with an import naming a file that appeared. That covers an import that fell back to a package (from . import m reaching __init__.py while m.py is missing) moving to the new file, and back. The dependency graph is therefore a function of the files on disk, the same as a fresh build would draw.

Searching while changes are applied

Searches and the watcher share one lock: searches read under it, install steps write. A writer that queues for such a lock makes it turn every new reader away until the writer has had its turn, and the writer gets its turn only when every running search has finished. So one slow search (a regex over a million files) and a one-file install step used to stop every other search for as long as the slow one ran, and searches still waiting after five seconds got “Index is currently being updated”.

Writers no longer queue. An install step (and each batch of the initial build) takes the write lock in a moment when no search holds it, trying every couple of milliseconds. While it waits, a new search holds back so the running ones can finish and the step can get in, and then goes ahead whether or not it did. It holds back a little longer than recent searches took (one and a half times the 90th percentile of the last 32), at least 250 ms and at most 2 s: the running searches need about that long to finish, and a search let in sooner keeps the step out too (see Catching up). A search therefore waits for a step’s short hold and a typical search’s length, never for one slow search. A search that arrives during a step waits for it (about a second at most); one still waiting after five seconds gets a 503 with Retry-After: 1 (gRPC: UNAVAILABLE), which fcs and the web UI retry on their own.

While changes wait to be applied, searches see the index as it was. They say so rather than pass it off as current: the index part of every search response carries pending_changes, the events the watcher has queued plus the files of the burst being applied, and the web UI shows “Index updating · N file changes to apply” beside the timing; fcs and the MCP tools print the same as a note.

Measured with examples/watch_load.rs --mixed on a generated tree of one million Python files (4 cores, four threads searching back to back with a mix of cheap, common-word and regex queries, the way the web handler runs them):

LoadVersionTurned awaySlowest searchp99MeanFiles applied
50,000 files rewritten at once, every 10 s, for 40 sbefore9 (3.0%)5.1 s4.3 s433 ms24,550
after01.5 s1.3 s684 ms138,739
200 files rewritten every 100 ms, for 30 sbefore4 (1.8%)5.0 s4.5 s511 ms10,643
after01.4 s1.1 s591 ms31,757

“Files applied” counts what the watcher had installed when the run ended: before, searches held it back and most of the changes were still waiting. The mean search is slower after (433 to 684 ms in the bulk run) for two reasons: the watcher now does three to six times the work in the same time on the same four cores, and a search holds back while a step waits. In exchange, no search waits seconds and none is turned away. “Files applied” can exceed the files rewritten: a file rewritten twice is installed twice, and in the bulk run the watcher’s event queue filled, so it reconciled the tree as well. Without that holding back the watcher did not get in once in a 30 s run on a 200,000-file tree, so nothing was applied at all.

Catching up

A step gets the lock only once the searches running at that moment have finished, so while people search each step costs about one search’s wait. With a fixed 512 files per step that made the watcher crawl: on the 200,000-file tree with four threads searching (about half a second per search), a change of 100,000 files was applied at about 400 files a second and was still 17,000 files behind four minutes after the change. The server looked as if it was updating for ever, with the “file changes to apply” count barely moving. Nobody searching, the same change applied at about 5,000 files a second.

A step now stays in about as long as it waited to get in, capped at one second: the watcher installs at least as much as it waits, so a backlog drains at half speed or better however busy the searches are, and a search waits at most about a second for a step. watch_load now keeps searching after the changes stop and measures how long until the index matches the disk again:

TreeChangeVersionIndex matches diskSlowest searchMean search
200,000 files100,000 rewritten at oncebeforenot after 240 s (17,200 files behind)2.2 s547 ms
after10 s after the change stopped2.1 s717 ms
1,000,000 files200,000 rewritten at onceafter214 s2.3 s866 ms

No search was turned away in any of these runs. The million-file run overflowed the watcher’s event queue twice, so it reconciled the whole tree as well (about 40 s of walking a million files, during which the pending count does not move); the rest is installing.

Holding back as long as a search runs

“Half speed or better” holds only while a step waits less than its one second cap, and that needs the running searches to finish without new ones taking their place. Until 0.19 a new search held back for a fixed 250 ms. With searches longer than that it went ahead while the earlier ones still ran, the step then waited for it as well, and so on: the step waited past its cap, held for only a second, and the drain fell far below half speed.

tests/lock_sim.rs shows it. It runs the real lock code and step policy on a simulated clock (fast_code_search::sim), so searches of any length and minutes of load run in about a second, the same schedule on every run and OS. Three made-up loads, the worst of five seeds each, with the one second cap:

Load (installing alone takes)Holding backDrained afterLongest search wait
4 people, 0.4–0.6 s searches back to back, 100,000 files (4.9 s)nonenot in 600 s23 ms
250 ms62.1 s1.25 s
500 ms10.1 s0.81 s
adaptive10.5 s1.64 s
1 search of 20 s and quick searches, 10 files250 ms20.0 s0.25 s
1 s20.1 s1.0 s
adaptive20.0 s0.25 s
12 people with quick searches, 2 with 1–4 s ones, 30,000 files (2.4 s)250 ms11.4 s1.14 s
500 ms8.6 s1.47 s
adaptive11.4 s1.14 s

Any fixed value works only for searches shorter than it, and the longer the value, the longer quick searches wait behind one slow one (second load). So a search holds back for one and a half times the 90th percentile of how long the last 32 searches held the index, between 250 ms and 2 s: on a large tree whose searches take half a second it holds back about 0.9 s, while one slow search among quick ones does not raise it. The factor is there because a search that holds back for less than the running ones take can get in before they end; with exactly the 90th percentile, the first load drained in up to 22 s. Before any search has finished, the hold-back is 250 ms, which is why the first step of the first load waits longer (the longest search wait in that row). Holding back only for a fixed window after the step starts to wait (instead of from each search’s arrival) did no better: in the first load the step never got in. The one second cap stays: with a 2 s cap the busy load drained in 6.6 s instead of 11.4 s, but a search then waited up to 2.1 s for a step; with 500 ms, quick searches waited less but the first load took 11.7 s. cargo test --release --test lock_sim -- --ignored --nocapture prints the full table (four caps and nine hold-back rules).

Files that can’t be read

A file can fail to read for a moment: an editor, a build or a virus scanner holds it open (a sharing violation on Windows), or its permissions change while it is rewritten. The initial build and the watcher tell this apart from a file that is simply not indexed (gone, too large, binary):

  • The build tries the file once more at its end. With watching on, the watcher then keeps trying it.
  • The watcher leaves the file’s entry as it was and tries again after 1 s, then 2, 4, 8 and 16 s. Until then the change counts as still to apply, so searches report it.
  • After six failed tries (about half a minute) the file is given up on: it is removed from the index, as ripgrep skips a file it cannot open, the server log names it, and every search says how many files could not be read. When it next changes and can be read, it is indexed and the count drops.

A failed read used to remove the file from the index without a word until it next changed.

When applying a batch fails

A panic while a batch is applied (a bug, or the disk changing under it in a way the code did not expect) is caught, so the watcher keeps running. The batch is not dropped: it is applied again a second later, one change at a time, and a change that fails again is removed from the index and reported as a file that could not be read. The batch used to be skipped, leaving its files out of date until each changed again.

Saving

After save_after_updates file changes the index is saved; it is also saved on shutdown when anything changed since the last save, so an edit made a second before Ctrl+C is on disk when the server starts again.

How this is tested

tests/watch_harness.rs runs the server’s own watch loop over a generated Python and TypeScript tree, changes it the way people and tools do (edits, editor-style atomic saves, renames across directories, directory renames and deletes, moves into excluded directories, bursts of dozens of changes, changes during the initial build), then compares the live index with a fresh index of the same disk: the file set, every hit for every vocabulary word, symbol definitions and dependency edges. The random sequences print their seed; replay one with FCS_WATCH_SEED=<seed> cargo test --test watch_harness. Another test holds a long search while a burst waits and checks that the step that waited installs more than one chunk. Another test searches continuously while a burst of changes is applied and fails if a search is turned away or waits too long; another holds a long search while a burst waits to be installed and checks that other searches are answered and report the changes still to apply.

tests/lifecycle_model.rs drives the same code (the watch loop’s apply_burst, the background indexer’s load and reconcile) through random sequences of edits, renames, folder moves, ignore-file changes, binary and oversized files, lost events, saves, restarts and crashes, and after every step compares the index with a naive model of the disk: the file set and every word’s hits, read page by page. A failure is shrunk to the shortest sequence that still fails. See How fcs is tested.

tests/fault_injection.rs makes reads fail and the install step panic on purpose, through fault points compiled in only with the failpoints feature (cargo test --features failpoints --test fault_injection), and checks that each failure is retried, counted while it lasts, and reported once given up on.

For larger trees, examples/watch_load.rs generates a tree of small scripts (a million, if you like), streams rewrites, creates and deletes into it through the real watch loop while threads search, and reports how many searches were answered or turned away and the latency percentiles. --mixed searches the way the web handler does, with queries that read most of the tree; a large --burst with a long --interval-ms is a bulk change such as a branch switch. After the changes stop it keeps searching until the index matches the disk again and reports how long that took, or “NOT SETTLED” after --settle-seconds; --index-path adds checkpoint saves:

cargo run --release --example watch_load -- --dir /tmp/fcs-load --files 1000000 \
    --burst 50000 --interval-ms 10000 --seconds 40 --mixed

Persistence

A saved index turns a restart into a load of a few seconds. The file format page describes the bytes; this page describes the behaviour.

Save

Saves are atomic and serialized: the engine’s read lock is held while the metadata, trigram directory and bitmaps are streamed into a uniquely named temporary file next to the target, fsynced, and renamed into place. Two saves cannot interleave, and a crash at any point leaves the previous file intact.

There are two kinds of save:

  • Checkpoints, while the server runs (during a long build, after it, and every save_after_updates changes), keep every file’s id: a file removed since the load is written as an empty slot. Nothing is renumbered, and a posting list no change touched is copied from the mapped file as its bytes. Afterwards the server reads the posting lists from the file it just wrote and frees the copies it held, so the file it loaded at startup is released.

  • The save at shutdown compacts: the empty slots go and every file after them is renumbered, so the file on disk has no gaps.

  • A save that is killed (kill -9, a power cut, the out-of-memory killer) leaves its temporary file (<index_path> with .tmp.<pid>.<n> in place of its extension, as large as the index). The next server to take the index lock removes them before it loads (“Removed temp files left by an index save that did not finish” in the log).

  • A failed rename (Windows refuses to replace a file another process has open) is tried once more with the old index moved aside; if that fails too, the old index is put back. The save fails, and the index on disk is the previous one.

  • A failed save (disk full) is logged as an error, and with save_after_updates the updates it held still count: the next save, after more updates or at shutdown, writes them. The index on disk is older until then, so the next start re-reads the files changed since.

tests/fault_injection.rs fails the write and the rename on purpose (fault points save.write and save.rename), and examples/chaos.rs kills the server binary during saves, builds and shutdowns and checks that the next start loads the index and catches up (see How fcs is tested).

One server per index

A server takes an exclusive lock on <index_path>.lock before it loads and records its PID and listen addresses in <index_path>.lock.owner. A second server started with the same index_path logs which process holds it, loads the index read-only and never saves, so two servers can no longer overwrite each other’s file. The lock is released when the process exits, however it exits.

Load

  1. Map the file, validate header, lengths, checksum and directory order.

  2. Stat pass: one stat per persisted file, in parallel. A file whose mtime or size differs is stale (a file that was read within 3 seconds of being modified is saved with no time, so it is always stale: on a file system with coarse timestamps it could have changed in the same tick); a file that is definitely gone (ENOENT) is removed; any other error keeps the entry, so a permission problem or an unmounted share cannot wipe a root from the checkpoint.

  3. Eligibility pass: every surviving file is checked against the current configuration and .gitignore rules (gitignore matchers cached per directory), so a rule change takes effect without a rebuild.

  4. Register files lazily (no reads), restore symbols, references and edges — or re-extract them from content if the file was written by an older extractor — and rebuild the fast-ranking metadata. Every saved file gets its saved position back as its id, and the stale and removed ones (and a checkpoint’s empty slots) are then tombstoned, as a removal at runtime is, so the posting lists are used as saved. (Renumbering the survivors instead rebuilt every posting list whenever a single file had changed, the largest cost of a load.) The gaps go at the save at shutdown.

    The posting lists are not decoded. The file stays mapped, and a query decodes only the lists for its own trigrams, about 13 µs per query at 302,000 files (a first page takes 2 to 86 ms). The lists stay in the page cache, which the OS can evict and share, instead of on the heap: at 302,000 files a loaded server holds 6 MB of posting data on the heap instead of about 479 MB, with 350 MB of the file mapped. The mapped lists still name the files removed or re-indexed since the save; those ids are kept in a small set the mapped lists are read without, so a removal costs one bit instead of a pass over every list. Files indexed after the load are held on the heap and united with the mapped lists until the next checkpoint writes them out. On Windows, which cannot replace a file that is mapped, the lists are decoded at load as before. If the lists had to be renumbered (a path saved twice), they are decoded too.

  5. Re-index the stale files that the current configuration and ignore files still allow, then discover anything new under the roots. Only the folders that changed are read. The last complete walk saved each folder’s modification time and a fingerprint of its ignore files (.gitignore, .ignore, .rgignore, .git/info/exclude). Adding, deleting or renaming an entry changes a folder’s time, so a folder whose time and ignore files match holds no new file. On restart every saved folder is stat’ed (in parallel). The changed ones are read one level deep, new folders are walked whole, and a folder whose ignore files changed is walked whole, since its rules apply below it. This is the trick git’s untracked cache uses. At 302,000 files (28,616 folders) it took 0.18 s where a full walk took 2.6 s with the file system cache warm, and 1.3 s where a full walk took 9.2 s with it cold.

    A folder whose time matches is still read when the index holds a different number of its files than the walk found eligible there. The stat pass only checks indexed files, so a file skipped for being too large, binary or unreadable would otherwise never be seen to change: a file that was too large and is then cut down rewrites its contents, not its folder. Such folders are read (one level) on every restart.

    A full walk happens instead when the configuration changed, the user’s global excludes file (core.excludesFile) changed, a root is new, or the index has no stamps yet: a fresh build saves them once its walk ends, and a checkpoint from a build that did not finish has none. A folder modified within 2 seconds of the walk is not stamped (a coarse clock could give a later change the same time), and neither is one the walk could not read; the folders above them are read again on the next restart, so it reaches them.

    Known limitation. This relies on the file system updating a folder’s modification time when an entry is added, removed or renamed, as local file systems do. On one that does not (some network or FUSE mounts), a file added while the server was stopped is found only when the watcher sees it change or its folder changes otherwise. Deleting the index file makes the next start walk everything.

Roots are compared in canonical form, so a symlinked temp directory on macOS or an 8.3 short name on Windows is the same root as its long spelling.

When a rebuild happens anyway

Only when the file’s format version differs from the server’s, older or newer (a downgrade rebuilds too). The message names the file; deleting it (or letting the server overwrite it) is all that is needed.

Memory and limits

What is resident

StructureGrows withNotes
Trigram posting listsdistinct trigrams × filesRoaring bitmaps; run-optimised after a build; after a load read in place from the mapped index file (page cache, not heap), except lists indexed since
Symbol tablesdefinitionsper file, restored from the saved index; plus a dictionary of distinct names, built after a build and saved with the index
Reference tablescall sites and type mentionsnames interned once; positions packed
Dependency graphresolved importsbidirectional, with cached dependent counts
File storefilesone small entry per file; content is not kept
Mapped contentfiles > 1 MiB read by searchesthe “mapped” figure in the UI; released when a file is removed

Small files are read into owned buffers per access and dropped afterwards, so search traffic does not accumulate memory. The latest results give the CI corpus’s resident memory after a build.

Where it goes

/api/diagnostics reports the index’s estimated heap per structure under index.memory, largest first, next to the process’s resident memory (index.resident_bytes, Linux only). corpus_bench prints the same breakdown after a build, and the scale scoreboard records it per scenario.

PartWhat it holds
postingstrigram posting lists (Roaring bitmaps) and their table
referencessymbol references and their interned names
symbolssymbol definitions and the name dictionary
importsimports as written, and those still waiting for a file to resolve to
graph_pathspath tables the import resolver looks paths up in
graph_edgesdependency edges and dependent counts
file_tableindexed paths and the path lookup
file_metadataranking metadata and display paths
dir_indexfiles by folder, for the watcher

The figures are estimates from lengths and capacities, not allocator measurements (src/heap_size.rs). tests/heap_estimates.rs keeps them honest: it counts every allocation and fails if the estimates for a loaded index fall outside 75% to 105% of the heap it really holds. Checked the same way on public code (at b1f9287). A loaded index reads its posting lists in place from the mapped index file, so they are not heap; the mapped bytes are reported apart (mapped_postings_bytes) and are page cache the system can evict and share:

CorpusIndexLive heapEstimatedPosting lists mapped
tokio + Django (6,200 files)just built75.2 MB46.4 MB (62%)none
tokio + Djangoloaded from disk19.4 MB18.7 MB (96%)12.6 MB
Linux kernel (85,700 files, 1.23 GB)just built547.9 MB505.3 MB (92%)none
Linux kernelloaded from disk286.3 MB282.3 MB (99%)149.3 MB

The Linux kernel, loaded from disk:

PartEstimated
symbols239.0 MB
graph_paths14.0 MB
file_table12.7 MB
file_metadata7.8 MB
postings (on the heap: the table and lists indexed since the load)4.0 MB
references3.2 MB
everything else1.7 MB

Each path is held once. The file store interns every file’s canonical path; the ranking metadata (its root-qualified path is an offset into that path), the import resolver’s path tables and the import lists all share that one allocation, so file_table counts the paths and the others count only their own tables. On the 302,000-file real corpus this took resident memory after a build from 1,665 MB to 1,504 MB (same machine, 3 rounds). The lowercase file-name stem is the exception: it is kept inside the metadata (up to 23 bytes; 85% of stems fit), because every candidate file of a search reads it and reading it through the path cost a cache miss per file.

Known limitations: on Windows the metadata keeps its own copy of the root-qualified path, since it is spelled with / and the stored path with \. A search with a path filter reads every candidate’s path from the file store: on an index just built (paths spread through the heap among the build’s other allocations) that measured 19% slower on 302,000 files (95 to 113 ms); on an index loaded from disk, where the paths are allocated in order, it is as fast as before.

A just-built index holds more than the same index loaded. On tokio + Django it is 75 MB against 19 MB. Most of the gap is the posting lists, which a loaded index reads from the mapped file. The rest is spare capacity: posting lists built one insert at a time keep it (a list in one container costs 168 bytes instead of 42), and the estimates cannot see it, which is why they cover only 62% there. A restart gives the memory back.

Resident memory is higher again. A build allocates and frees far more than the index it leaves behind (file contents, per-batch trigram sets, merge buffers), and the allocator keeps the freed pages. On Linux (glibc) the server hands them back to the system once a build or a load is done (malloc_trim), outside the write lock:

CorpusResident beforeAfterTook
tokio + Django135 MB107 MB8 ms
Linux kernel793 MB709 MB36 ms
Linux + LLVM + Rust + Kubernetes (302,000 files)1,718 MB1,613 MBnot timed

What is left above the live heap is fragmentation: freed memory that shares pages with live data. Compacting the posting lists after a build (reallocating each at its exact size) was tried. It did not lower resident memory and made finalize about 200 ms slower on tokio + Django, so it is not done. corpus_bench shows everything outside the estimates as “not estimated”.

Known limitation: on macOS, Windows and musl builds the freed memory is not handed back, so resident memory after a build stays higher there than after a restart. The breakdown leaves out the allocator, thread stacks, mapped content, the regex cache and spare capacity inside posting lists, so it reads low, most of all just after a build. Use it to see which part grew between two runs, not as an exact total.

Limits that protect the server

LimitValueWhere
Page size1 to search.max_page_size (1,000); default search.default_page_size (50)max
Offset≤ search.max_page_offset (100,000)offset; deeper pages are refused
Files readbest first; search.fast_rank_files (no limit by default), up to search.max_fast_rank_files on requestmax_files
Match budgetsearch.min_match_budget (32,768) to search.max_match_budget (100,000), search.match_budget_multiplier (8) × page end; about 230 bytes per match while a search runsstops the scan; reported as truncated_by_budget and limits_hit
Deadlinetimeout_ms or just under request_timeout_secsthe scan stops on its own even if the HTTP response was abandoned
Context lines≤ search.max_context_lines (10) per hit (/api/context: ≤ 200)context
Concurrent searchesmax_concurrent_searches, shared by REST and gRPCbeyond it: 503 / RESOURCE_EXHAUSTED immediately
Regexcompiled size and DFA size limitsabsurd patterns are a 400
Per-file hitssearch.per_file_matches (1,000) per document per search, its best by score; the budget from result 1000 onkeeps a broad regex bounded
File sizemax_file_size (default 10 MB)larger files are not indexed
WebSocket frames4 KiB inbound/ws/progress ignores inbound data

When a limit cuts a search short, the response names it in limits_hit (max_files, match_budget, per_file_cap, timeout or indexing, each with a sentence for people). gRPC sends the names in the fcs-limits-hit response header, fcs prints them on stderr, and MCP adds a “Results capped” line, so a capped result never passes for a complete one.

None of these need tuning for normal use; they exist so a bad query or an abandoned client cannot pin the machine. The defaults are measured; see Search limits. MCP’s own limits are in [mcp] (search_code 50 hits by default, at most 1,000, paged with offset).

How fcs is tested

fcs makes a few promises that a wrong answer breaks quietly: a search shows every match, in rank order, and finds what ripgrep finds; the index follows the disk through edits, branch switches, lost events and restarts; searches are answered while the index changes; the import graph, symbols and references are what graph support and reference support say. A missing hit looks like an empty result, not an error, so each promise has a test whose answer comes from somewhere other than fcs’s own code: ripgrep, a language’s own resolver, a deliberately naive model, or a generator that wrote every line and knows the answer.

The suites

SuiteChecksAgainstRun
Unit tests (src/**)each part on its ownhand-written casescargo test --lib
tests/integration_tests.rsthe server end to end, saving and loadinghand-written casescargo test
tests/frontend_parity.rsweb API, gRPC and fcs give the same hits in the same ordereach othercargo test
tests/watch_harness.rsthe watch loop over scripted and random editsa fresh index of the same diskcargo test
tests/lifecycle_model.rsrandom sequences of edits, renames, folder moves and deletes, ignore files, binary and oversized files, lost events, saves, restarts and crashes; after every step: the file set, every word’s hits read page by page (no repeats, each file’s hits together), the index’s self-checksa naive model (a map of files, a hand-written ignore matcher, a line-by-line search)cargo test --test lifecycle_model
tests/search_properties.rsliteral (-i, -s, -w) and regex searches on random text with case-folding oddities and multi-byte charactersrgcargo test --test search_properties (needs rg)
tests/python_codebase.rsthe import graph, symbol search, reference search and strict -deps:/-rdeps: scopes on generated Python trees, after a build and after the watcher rewrites the treethe generator’s answer key, itself checked by Python and grimpcargo test --test python_codebase
tests/contract_walk.rsone search walked to its last hit through every surface: REST at several page sizes and random offsets, gRPC, MCP over HTTP and fcs mcp, fcs (piped, -l, offline) and the web page in headless Chromium with LOAD MORE and “Show N more hits” pressed until they are gone; no hit twice or missing, each file’s hits together, files by best hit, honest countsthe engine’s exhaustive list, itself checked against the files on disk and the generator’s reference keycargo test --test contract_walk (the web page needs Playwright, see below)
tests/rank_snapshot.rsthe first 20 hits and the total of about a hundred searches; the same corpus at two places on disk must rank the sametests/snapshots/rank_order.txt, changed only on purposecargo test --test rank_snapshot
tests/discovery_oracle.rswhich files are indexed (the build and the watcher’s per-file check) on random trees of .gitignore, .ignore, .rgignore and .git/info/exclude rules; the FILES box with a regexrg --files, and rg over the path listcargo test --test discovery_oracle (needs rg)
tests/lock_sim.rsthe engine lock and the watcher’s install steps under minutes of made-up load (back-to-back half-second searches, one 20 s search among quick ones, a busy server during a branch switch): every search answered, no search waits past its hold-back plus one step, backlogs drain at half speed or better, a slow search delays only the writerbounds derived from the protocol, on a simulated clock (fast_code_search::sim): the real lock code, the same schedule on every run and OS, four minutes in about a secondcargo test --test lock_sim (-- --ignored --nocapture prints the tuning table)
tests/fault_injection.rsfailed reads (permission, out of file handles, a file gone between listing and reading), a slow disk, panics while reading, parsing or applying changes, lost changes and lost events, a save onto a full disk or a failed rename: each is retried, reported or repaired, searches are not held up, and the saved index stays wholeexpected recoverycargo test --features failpoints --test fault_injection (fault points are listed in src/faults.rs)
examples/rg_backtest.rsfixed and generated queries and regexes on tokio, Django and fixtures, with paging and default limits, on an index built in one go, restarted from disk (--history reload) and filled by the watcher (--history watch)rgsee Correctness against ripgrep
examples/graph_oracle.rsthe import graph on Django, Vite, generated deep trees and generated monorepo layouts (projects side by side with their own src/, a root folder that is a package, a split namespace package, npm workspaces with tsconfig paths and extends); every edge missed or wrong must be a known gap listed with its reason and countgrimp, TypeScript’s resolverCI job “graph oracle”, gaps in benches/graph/known_gaps.toml
examples/ref_oracle.rs, examples/impact_oracle.rsPython references and diff impact on real repositoriesJediCI jobs “ref oracle”, “impact oracle”
examples/scoreboard.rsthe server binary over HTTP in seven scenarios (cold build, warm restart, branch switch, ten-minute stream, slow mix, saves under load, event-queue overflow): searches turned away, latency, time until the index matches the disk, memory, CPU, diagnostic captures, crashes, /api/checktargets in benches/scoreboard/targets.tomlnightly and on pull requests that touch locking or watching (workflow “Scale scoreboard”), see Scale scoreboard
examples/chaos.rskill -9 of the server binary at random moments during a build, a load, a stream of edits, a periodic save, a burst and the shutdown save; after each, a restart must load the saved index (never reject it), leave no temp file, catch up with every change and pass /api/checkthe files on disknightly and on pull requests that touch saving or watching (workflow “Scale scoreboard”, job “Kill and restart”), see below
examples/watch_load.rs, examples/limits_bench.rssearches answered and latency while a large tree changes; where limits bitemeasured numbersby hand, see Incremental updates

Every test that drives the index also runs its self-checks (check_invariants: posting lists name only live files, path lookups and the folder index agree, caches hold nothing for removed files, the graph’s two directions agree), the same checks fcs check --live and the server’s background scrub run (see Checking a live index).

The generated Python codebases

tests/support/pygen.rs writes packages nested up to four deep, each with an __init__.py and modules, from a seed. Modules import each other in every form graph support lists (absolute, aliased, several on one line, parenthesised lists, one to three dots, imports inside functions and under if TYPE_CHECKING:; cycles happen) and use what they import in every position reference support lists as found: calls, attribute calls, await, instantiation, obj.m(), Cls().m(), Cls.m(x), super().m(), decorators, base classes, annotations (list[C], C | None, Optional[C]), raise/except, and values in lists, dicts, keyword arguments and comparisons. Names also appear where they are not references (strings, comments, docstrings, string annotations). Every name is unique, so “the references of fn_0042” has one right answer, which the generator recorded as it wrote the line.

Before the key is trusted, Python compiles every file and grimp must draw exactly the key’s edges, line for line. Then the test rewrites the tree as another seed’s and hands the changes to the watcher in a few bursts: the incremental result must match the new key as a fresh build does.

Rank order

Everything above checks which hits a search finds and that a list is well formed; none notices when a change quietly reorders the first page, the page people read. tests/rank_snapshot.rs pins it: the first 20 hits of about a hundred searches (words, generated names, regexes, symbols, references) are kept in tests/snapshots/rank_order.txt. A ranking change is meant to move hits, so a difference is not a failure to fix but a change to accept on purpose:

FCS_UPDATE_SNAPSHOTS=1 cargo test --test rank_snapshot

and the snapshot’s diff then shows in the pull request what moved.

The web page

The contract walk’s browser leg runs scripts/e2e/web_walk.cjs with Playwright. Locally:

npm install --no-save --prefix /tmp/pw playwright@1.56.1
/tmp/pw/node_modules/.bin/playwright install chromium
NODE_PATH=/tmp/pw/node_modules FCS_REQUIRE_BROWSER=1 cargo test --release --test contract_walk

Without Playwright the leg prints a note and is skipped; CI’s Linux test job sets FCS_REQUIRE_BROWSER, so there it fails instead.

Killed at any moment

examples/chaos.rs starts the server binary on a generated tree of 20,000 files and kills it with SIGKILL, round after round, at a random moment of one of these:

KindKilled
buildduring a build from scratch, which saves a checkpoint every quarter of the tree
loadwhile it loads the saved index and checks the tree
streamwhile 300 files a second are rewritten, created and removed, and saved every 300 updates; the edits go on for a second after the kill
save0 to 20 ms after one of those saves begins (it watches for the save’s temp file every millisecond)
burstwhile the watcher installs a tenth of the files rewritten at once
shutdown0 to 20 ms after the save at shutdown (SIGTERM) begins

After each kill it starts the server again and requires: the saved index is loaded, or there was none (a kill must never leave one the next start rejects); no temp file of a killed save is left once the server answers; the server becomes ready and catches up (nothing pending, the changed files indexed as they are on disk); /api/check?all=true finds nothing; and it does not crash. The seed is printed, and --seed N replays the same kill moments; a failing round keeps its server log as failed-<round>.log.

60 rounds on a 4 vCPU VM, 2026-10-23 (seed 4):

Killed duringRoundsKilled mid saveNo index afterIndex rejectedTemp files leftSlowest readySlowest catch-upCheck problems
build1013001.8 s1.8 s0
load1000000.3 s0.5 s0
stream1010000.4 s0.8 s0
save10100000.3 s0.8 s0
burst1000000.3 s0.8 s0
shutdown10100000.4 s0.8 s0

“No index after” is a build killed before its first checkpoint: the next start builds from scratch. A build killed later loads the checkpoint and indexes the rest. CI runs 40 rounds on pull requests (seed 1) and 100 nightly (a new seed).

This tests a killed process, not a power cut: the kernel still writes out what the process wrote. A save fsyncs its file before the rename and, on Linux and macOS, the folder after it, so a power cut should leave either the old index or the new one; nothing here simulates that.

Reproducing a failure

  • proptest suites print the shrunk sequence or query that failed, which is usually enough to write a named test (the lifecycle model keeps the ones it found that way). PROPTEST_RNG_SEED=N replays a run and PROPTEST_CASES=N runs more: PROPTEST_CASES=3000 cargo test --release --test lifecycle_model.
  • FCS_PYGEN_SEEDS=1,2,3 picks the generated trees (FCS_PYGEN_PACKAGES their size) and FCS_PYGEN_KEEP=DIR writes them to DIR to look at.
  • FCS_WATCH_SEED=N replays a random watch-harness run.

On pull requests CI uses fixed seeds, so a pull request is not failed by a case it did not cause; pushes to main use new seeds each time and keep exploring. CI runs the lifecycle model 600 times, the search properties 2,000 times and the discovery oracle 1,000 times (job “fcs vs ripgrep”, which also runs the backtest on a reloaded and a watched index) and 40 generated Python trees with grimp (job “graph oracle”).

What these tests found

  • Files with no trigram (two bytes in x.py) were never searched by short queries (self-checks).
  • extra_binary_extensions was documented but rejected (self-checks).
  • The watcher indexed folders ignored by a parent’s .gitignore when something happened in them, and a restart re-indexed changed files that were now ignored (lifecycle model).
  • A file rewritten in the same timestamp tick with the same size could stay stale across a restart on coarse file systems (found while writing the lifecycle model; now covered by a unit test).
  • The watcher’s per-file ignore check disagreed with the build and with ripgrep in three ways: a rule deeper down (!a/b/ after /a) brought files back from an ignored folder; the nearest ignore file won even when a parent’s .ignore should beat a .gitignore next to the file; and rules above a nested repository were dropped inside it (discovery oracle).
  • Searches held back a fixed 250 ms for a waiting install step, too short for half-second searches: the watcher drained a backlog at 8% of its speed, not the “half speed or better” the docs claimed (lock simulation; see Incremental updates).
  • After every restart from a saved index, every search waited 4 to 5 s while the import graph’s parked imports were retried in one lock hold; a backlog diagnostic fired under steady edits the watcher kept up with (scale scoreboard).
  • A save killed half way left a temp file as large as the index, for good; a save that failed (disk full) was forgotten, so the shutdown skipped its save; a failed rename deleted the index before trying again; and a panic while reading a file during the build left it out without a word (fault points and the kill-and-restart run).
  • Earlier probes found a crash when a large file was rewritten during a search, “server unavailable” at the end of a build, and lost changes after failed reads or a panic; the fault and watch tests cover them.

Benchmark methodology

Every number this project publishes comes from a run you can open, on a machine whose specification is recorded with the result. Four tiers:

TierCorpusWhat it measuresWhere it runs
Instruction countstokio + Django (below), under callgrindinstructions per operation: build, each query, incremental update, save, load; fails on 3% more than the last passing pushevery push to main
Synthetic (Criterion)generated Rust-like source, 50 lines per file, 50–1,000 filesthe query path in isolation: text, regex, filters, paging; index build; save and loadlocally (cargo bench); in CI until October 2026
Real corpustokio 1.45.0 + Django 5.2: 6,237 files, 38.9 MBfull build, resident memory, save and load, p50/p95 per query kind, incremental updateevery push to main
Large corpusrust-lang/rust 1.89.0file count, throughput, memory at scalenightly

Runs live in the Benchmarks workflow; the real-corpus tier is charted per commit at dev/bench by github-action-benchmark.

Instruction counts

Wall time on a shared runner moves by tens of percent between days, so a 5% slowdown hides in it. An instruction count barely moves: the same binary on the same tree executes the same instructions on any x86 runner. scripts/bench/icount.sh runs corpus_bench --icount under callgrind, which counts every thread’s instructions between two marker calls around each operation, and compares the counts with the last ones a passing push published (dev/bench/results/icount.json on the site). An operation over 3% more fails the run, and main-health opens a main-red issue.

Counts are published only from a passing run, so a failure stays red until the added work is removed or accepted. Accepting is a decision, named in the squash commit message of the change that adds it:

Accept-instructions: index_build, text/common

(all accepts every operation.) The workflow passes those names to icount.sh --accept, the run passes, and its counts become the new base.

What counts do not see: cache misses, memory bandwidth, I/O, lock waits and how well work spreads over cores. A change that makes code faster by doing the same work in parallel (B2 did) shows no gain here, and one that makes it slower by contention shows no loss. The same-machine A/B (scripts/bench/ab.sh) stays the measure a PR quotes; counts catch added work between A/Bs. Counts are taken with one rayon thread, which counts the same work without the cost of spreading it, and each query is counted five times (median). Run-to-run noise on one commit, measured on 2026-10-25 over eight runs: 0.5% or less for every operation except symbol/exact and symbol/prefix, which moved by up to 4.2% for a reason not yet found; those two fail only at 10%. On the GitHub runner the count takes about 10 minutes, about what the Criterion suites it replaced took: the first run (40246218157) finished the whole job in 18 minutes, against 20 for the run before it.

How queries are timed

  • The index is built once; queries run against it warm, 50 results per page, after a warm-up iteration. p50 and p95 are over repeated invocations of the same query.
  • “Common” is a word present in most files (return); “identifier” a name present in a few; “no match” a string in none (which measures candidate rejection alone).
  • Regex cases: a pattern with a required literal, a case-insensitive one, and one with no literal (a full scan).
  • The incremental case rewrites one file and applies the change through the same path the watcher uses.

What the machine is
