GitHub-hosted ubuntu-latest runners: 4 vCPU, 16 GB RAM, SSD, shared tenancy. Absolute numbers on your hardware will differ; the value of the CI numbers is that they are comparable commit to commit. Local runs are labelled as such wherever they appear.

Reproduce

cargo run --release --example corpus_bench -- path/to/repo [more paths] --label mine
scripts/bench/icount.sh                     # instruction counts (needs valgrind, ~16 min)
cargo bench                                 # synthetic tier
cargo bench --bench search_benchmark        # search only

corpus_bench prints the same table CI does (--markdown) and can emit bencher lines for tracking (--bencher).

Reading the trend charts

The trend page charts every measurement per commit to main. Each chart is a cost, so lower is better; the vertical axis is time per operation in the unit that fits the chart (or bytes for memory). The shaded band is that run’s spread: median to 95th percentile for the real-corpus rows (Criterion’s reported deviation for the synthetic rows recorded before October 2026). A step on every chart at the same commit is a change of runner, not of code. Each chart carries a sentence saying what it measures and what would move it.

Ranking quality

Latency says how fast the engine is; this says whether what it puts first is what a reader wanted. benches/ranking_quality.toml holds labelled queries over the pinned corpora — text, symbols, regex and references — each naming the file and line that should come first (struct Runtime → the definition in runtime.rs, not a doc comment; Field in symbols mode → the model field, not FieldOverridePost in a test). The suite reports precision@1 (the expected result is the first hit) and precision@5, runs on every push, and fails the run below 0.90 / 0.95, so a change that makes searches faster but worse cannot land. Its first run scored 0.30 and surfaced six ranking defects; see the changelog.

cargo run --release --example ranking_quality -- bench-corpus/tokio bench-corpus/django
cargo run --release --example ranking_quality -- … --explain 'struct Runtime'   # why a query ranks as it does

Correctness against ripgrep

Fast and well ranked is worthless if a match is missing. A backtest runs 144 searches through fcs and through ripgrep on the same trees and fails on any line one finds and the other does not; see Correctness against ripgrep for how it works and how to extend it.

Comparison with scan tools

scripts/bench/compare.sh <dir> times the same queries through ripgrep, ugrep (if installed) and fcs against a server on the same tree, warm cache, median of seven runs, and records the machine. It reports both the fcs client’s wall time (process start plus HTTP) and the engine’s own time. The point is not that a scan tool is slow — it is the right tool for a one-off search — but that an index answers in the same time however large the tree is, and ranks. Results are on the latest results page when the comparison job has run.

Against ripgrep and ugrep

A scan tool reads every file on every query. An index reads the tree once and answers from memory. This page puts the two side by side on the same machine, the same tree and the same queries, and says plainly what the numbers do and do not mean. The script is scripts/bench/compare.sh; the benchmark workflow runs it on every push and the result appears on the latest results page.

How it is measured

  • The same query string goes to every tool. Literal rows use ripgrep’s fixed-string, case-insensitive mode, which is what a plain fcs search does; regex rows are given to both as regular expressions.
  • Warm cache, median of seven runs, wall time from process start to exit, every tool’s output written to the same scratch file. fcs asks for the best 50 hits; the scan tools are given no match limit, because a scan tool cannot know the best 50 without reading everything anyway.
  • Output goes to a file, not /dev/null, because ugrep (like GNU grep) detects a /dev/null stdout and quietly switches to “stop at the first match”. The first CI run timed that as a 6 ms “scan” of forty megabytes.
  • fcs (client) is the command-line client talking to a server over HTTP. It includes starting the process and the round trip. fcs (engine) is the time the server reports for the search itself, which is what an editor holding a connection sees.
  • The server is started fresh on the tree and timing begins only after its build has finished. Build time is not in the table; it is on the latest results page.
  • The script refuses to run on a FUSE, NFS or SMB mount. On an external NTFS drive the same ripgrep query took about one second instead of forty milliseconds, and publishing that would have been dishonest.

Results on the CI runner

From run 40411254740 on commit b1f9287 (2026-10-26, version 0.19.0): GitHub-hosted ubuntu-latest, AMD EPYC 7763, 4 vCPU, ripgrep 14.1.0, ugrep 5.0.0.

tokio 1.45.0 + Django 5.2, 7,601 files.

Queryripgrepugrepfcs (client)fcs (engine)
common word return71.3 ms94.2 ms31.4 ms30.75 ms
identifier Config61.3 ms89.6 ms13.6 ms10.53 ms
rare identifier xq9zv_nothing_here57.6 ms89.7 ms4.9 ms0.02 ms
two words fn main73.5 ms86.8 ms8.6 ms3.79 ms
regex with literal fn\s+\w+\(81.6 ms115.9 ms33.3 ms31.39 ms
regex, no literal [a-z]+_[a-z]+_[a-z]+\(83.0 ms131.9 ms44.1 ms44.39 ms
case-insensitive (?i)unwrap\(59.4 ms86.0 ms9.6 ms4.88 ms

The latest results page shows the newest run. Hand-run laptop tables from before v0.17.0 used to sit here, with the engine near 1 ms on every row. They were removed because v0.17.0 deliberately made queries with many matches slower (see below), so they no longer describe fcs.

Reading the numbers

  • ripgrep is fast. Sixty-odd milliseconds on a 4-vCPU runner for a full scan of seven thousand files is remarkable, and for a one-off search from a shell it is the right tool. Nothing here argues otherwise.
  • ugrep is in the CI run. On the GitHub runner (4 vCPUs) the first two runs put ugrep at 6 ms against ripgrep’s 60 ms on every query that had a match, and at 84 ms on the one that had none. That was ugrep’s /dev/null shortcut above, not a scan; the latest results page shows the corrected run. It is a good reminder of why the script and the raw JSON are published with the numbers.
  • The engine’s time follows the matches, not the tree. The trigram index narrows every query to the files that can match before any text is read, so a rare identifier costs microseconds however large the tree is. A word that matches thousands of lines costs tens of milliseconds, because fcs reads and ranks up to 32,768 matches (the match budget) before it returns the best fifty. Before v0.17.0 the budget was 512 and those rows took about a millisecond, but page one held only 10% of the exhaustive top 50 instead of 81%; see search limits. A scan tool’s time grows with every byte in the tree; the engine’s stops growing at the budget.
  • A query per keystroke changes the arithmetic. An editor or web UI that searches as you type issues ten queries for a ten-letter identifier. As the identifier gets longer it matches fewer lines, so later keystrokes cost a few milliseconds or less, while a scan pays the full read every time. This, and not the single-query gap, is why the project is a server.
  • The fcs client column is mostly process start. About five milliseconds of each row are the OS starting a process and one HTTP request. A client that keeps a connection, such as the web UI or an editor extension, sees the engine column.
  • Scan tools do not rank. Both tools return matching lines; only one of them puts the definition of Runtime first. That is measured separately on the ranking quality page.
  • Zoekt is the closest comparable design — a trigram index behind a server — and a comparison against it is planned once the corpus and query set are shared between the two; see the roadmap.

Reproduce it

cargo build --release --bins
scripts/bench/compare.sh path/to/tree [more trees] --runs 7 --json compare.json --markdown compare.md

rg must be on the path; ugrep is used if present. The tree must be on a local filesystem.

Ranking quality

Speed is easy to measure and easy to game: an engine that returns the first fifty lines containing the query is fast and useless. This page tracks the thing that matters — does the first result answer the query? — with a suite of labelled queries that runs on every push and fails the build when it regresses.

What is measured

Each query in benches/ranking_quality.toml names the file (and, for text queries, the line) a reader would want first, with a one-line reason. The corpora are the pinned tokio 1.45.0 and Django 5.2 trees used by the latency benchmark, so both measurements describe the same index.

  • precision@1 — the share of queries whose first result is the expected one.
  • precision@5 — the share whose expected result is in the top five.

The CI gate is precision@1 ≥ 0.90 and precision@5 ≥ 0.95. The suite deliberately favours the cases a scan tool gets wrong: a definition that has many mentions in docs and tests, a bare identifier whose definition should beat its uses, a short name (Field) with several unrelated definitions where the widely imported one should win, and a reference query where the definition itself must not appear.

Current score

tokio 1.45.0 + Django 5.2: precision@1 1.00, precision@5 1.00 over 20 queries.

QueryModeExpected firstWhyRank found

What it caught

The first run of the suite scored 0.30 at precision@1. Every miss was a ranking defect, not a missing feature, and each fix is in the changelog:

  • Items declared inside cfg_if! and similar macro bodies were invisible to the symbol extractor, so struct Runtime ranked a doc comment above the definition. The extractor now re-parses macro bodies.
  • Lines that matched only some of a multi-term query could outrank the line with the whole phrase. Phrase matches are now a strict tier above all-terms and single-term lines.
  • Test, example, mock and fixture paths carried the same weight as shipped code. They are now scaled by 0.6, and public definitions by 1.6.
  • Symbol search compared the query against names with visibility and attribute prefixes still attached, so pub struct Mutex lost to pub(crate) struct MutexGuard. Modifiers are stripped before scoring.
  • Symbol search over a large candidate set spent its per-file budget on the first files scanned in parallel, which made results depend on thread scheduling. Candidates are now scored from the symbol cache first and only the best files are scanned for matches.
  • Match budgets were consumed by whichever files finished first, so the same query could return different results on different runs. Files that contain the whole phrase are now scanned in a separate pass before the rest.
  • Reference hits all carried the same score, so ties were broken by file id, which follows discovery order: the suite scored 1.00 on a laptop and 0.95 on the CI runner, where a test’s call to get_object_or_404 came first. References now take the file’s score (dependency boost, test-path penalty) and all ties break on the path.

Running it

git clone --depth 1 --branch tokio-1.45.0 https://github.com/tokio-rs/tokio bench-corpus/tokio
git clone --depth 1 --branch 5.2 https://github.com/django/django bench-corpus/django
cargo run --release --example ranking_quality -- bench-corpus/tokio bench-corpus/django

Add --explain 'struct Runtime' (with --explain-mode symbols|regex|references for other modes) to print the top results with their scores, tiers and the factors that produced them. --min-p1 and --min-p5 turn the run into a gate, as CI does. Adding a query is a five-line TOML entry; the point of the suite is that every ranking change comes with the example that motivated it.

Correctness against ripgrep

A search tool that misses a line is worse than a slow one: nobody notices the line that was not shown. Unit tests check the cases someone thought of. The backtest checks fcs against an independent, widely used tool on real code, and runs on every change to the engine.

The idea

ripgrep is the oracle. For each search in a fixed query set, the backtest:

  1. runs the search through the fcs engine with every result limit switched off,
  2. runs the same search through rg with the flags that mean the same thing,
  3. compares the two answers line by line.

A line ripgrep reports and fcs does not is a miss. A line fcs reports and ripgrep does not is an extra. Either one fails the run, unless the query is marked known with a written reason.

The translation to rg flags comes from the query alone, never from fcs’s own matching code, so a bug in fcs cannot hide by also being in the oracle. Where fcs syntax has no rg equivalent (file:, lang:), the query states what it means in rg terms, as -g globs written by hand.

ripgrep was chosen over grep and git grep because it shares fcs’s regex engine and Unicode case folding, so any difference is a real difference. See Matching compared with grep and ripgrep for where fcs, ripgrep, grep and git grep behave differently, and why.

Generated queries

The fixed set holds the cases someone thought of. --generate N adds N queries made up from the corpus itself: identifiers, substrings, whole lines, punctuation runs and non-ASCII text, each in random case, some with MATCH CASE and whole word set. Each runs through fcs and through rg -F (with -i and -w as set) and the lines must match. A query that differs is shrunk, dropping characters from either end while it still differs, and the report ends with the smallest forms as [[query]] blocks to add to benches/rg_backtest.toml with the fix.

CI generates 500 per run: with a fixed seed on pull requests, and a new seed on every push to main and once a week, so the set keeps exploring. Rerun a failure locally with the seed from the report:

cargo run --release --example rg_backtest -- bench-corpus/tokio bench-corpus/django \
    tests/fixtures/backtest --generate 500 --seed SEED

Its first runs found two whole-word bugs, both now fixed and in the query set: a combining mark (an Indic vowel sign, a decomposed accent) was not counted as a word character, and with MATCH CASE a match rejected by the whole-word test skipped the overlapping one after it (}} in field}}}).

On your own code: fcs check

The backtest and the graph oracles run on pinned open-source trees. fcs check runs the same checks on the folders you index, so a gap that only your code exposes shows up too:

fcs check                      # the configuration's paths, else the current folder
fcs check ~/src/app --seed 42  # one folder; repeat a run with its seed

It indexes the folders afresh, in memory, with your configuration’s settings, then:

  1. makes up 200 queries from your files (--queries N) and compares each with rg -F, as above; any query that differs is shrunk and reported with the lines only one side found;
  2. compares the import graph with grimp for Python and with TypeScript’s own resolver for JavaScript and TypeScript, when those are installed (pip install grimp; typescript in the repository’s node_modules), grouping the missed edges by the kind of import. See Dependency graph support for which misses are known gaps.

The report is markdown on stdout, progress on stderr. The exit status is 1 when a search differs from ripgrep: that is a bug, please report it with the shrunk query. Graph differences are reported but do not fail the run. --no-search and --no-graph skip either half.

Its first run, on Django, found a real bug: case-insensitive search did not fold the long ſ to s (or the micro sign µ to μ) as ripgrep does. Search now uses Unicode simple case folding throughout (src/search/fold.rs, checked against regex-syntax’s tables for every character).

How a query is translated

fcs queryripgrep runCompared how
Text (Runtime, fn main, "x":, -Wall)rg -F -i -e TEXT, the text as typed minus operatorsthe same lines exactly
case:yesdrops -ithe same lines exactly
word:yesadds -wthe same lines exactly
file: / lang:the query’s own globs (-g)the same lines exactly
Regexrg -e PATTERN, plus -U when the pattern spans linesthe line where each match starts

rg always runs with --no-config -uu --crlf, one tree at a time. Only files fcs indexed are compared, so differences in which files get searched (size limits, excluded folders, binary files) never show up as search differences. File-name hits have no rg counterpart and are left out.

What it runs on

  • Real code: tokio 1.45.0 and Django 5.2, the same pinned trees as the speed benchmarks. About 6,000 files of Rust and Python.
  • Edge cases: tests/fixtures/backtest, small files that real repositories rarely contain. They include CRLF line endings, a file with no final newline, a file with 250 matches, a very long line, Unicode (Greek, German, Japanese, emoji), quotes, operator-like text, a byte order mark and a vertical tab.
  • Cases borrowed from other tools: tests/fixtures/backtest/mined holds 37 cases adapted from ripgrep’s regression tests (tests/regression.rs, named after the issue they fixed) and Zoekt’s index tests (index/index_test.go). These are the kinds of patterns a trigram index or regex literal extractor tends to get wrong: one- and two-byte terms, trigrams present but in the wrong order or split across files, optional groups and {0} repeats, character classes, flags changed mid-pattern, word boundaries next to punctuation, and matches that start on a newline.

The query set, benches/rg_backtest.toml, has 126 queries. Each one says what it exercises (why). They cover literals, short terms, quotes, case and whole-word options, Unicode, path and language filters, phrases, leading dashes and regex.

Beyond matching

Three more checks run on every query:

  • Repeatable. The first page (50 results) is fetched three times and must be identical. A search that stops at its match budget must still give the same answer every time.
  • LOAD MORE (--check-paging, on in CI). Each query with up to 1000 matches is loaded the way the web UI’s LOAD MORE does it: the whole list again, 50 longer each time, while the server says more exist. Every match must end up shown exactly once.
  • Default limits (--check-defaults, on in CI). Everything above runs without result limits, so it cannot see a problem the limits themselves cause. This check walks each query with up to 20,000 matches (--defaults-max-hits) the way a web UI user would with the server’s default settings, once with Auto ranking and once with Fast: the first page, SEARCH ALL FILES if ranking skipped files, then LOAD MORE until the server says there is no more (the whole list again up to 1000, then 50 at a time by offset). Every match must end up shown exactly once, and a total given as an exact count must be the real one whenever every candidate file was searched.

Running it

# what CI runs (the corpora are shallow clones; see .github/workflows/backtest.yml)
cargo run --release --example rg_backtest -- bench-corpus/tokio bench-corpus/django \
    tests/fixtures/backtest --check-paging --check-defaults --markdown report.md --json report.json

# just the edge cases: no clones needed, a few seconds
cargo run --release --example rg_backtest -- tests/fixtures/backtest

# only queries whose text contains "needle"
cargo run --release --example rg_backtest -- tests/fixtures/backtest --only needle

The report lists every query with ripgrep’s and fcs’s line counts, and shows sample lines for each miss or extra. The run exits non-zero on any difference that is not marked known. CI runs it on every pull request that touches src/, the query set or the fixtures, and on every push to main. The report appears in the job summary.

Keeping it honest

  • Every search bug fixed gets a query. When a search bug is fixed, add the query that showed it to benches/rg_backtest.toml, with a fixture file if the corpora do not already contain the case. Then it stays fixed.
  • A difference is fixed or explained, never hidden. If fcs differs from ripgrep on purpose, mark the query known = "…" with the reason, and list it under “Known differences” in Matching compared with grep and ripgrep. The backtest still reports it and says when it stops differing, so a stale known is noticed.
  • The oracle stays independent. Never derive rg flags or globs from fcs code. Write them out in the query.

What it has caught

When it was first run, the backtest found that a broad search returned a different first page on almost every run, and that LOAD MORE repeated some results and skipped others. It also found that Greek words with a final sigma were missed, that a regex able to match nothing reported a line past the end of a file, and that a byte order mark stopped ^ from matching line 1. All five are fixed (see the changelog).

The default-limits check was added after fast ranking’s 2,000-file cap and the old offset ceiling were found by hand, not by the backtest. On its first run it found that LOAD MORE past 1000 results repeated some matches and skipped others on broad queries (self.assertEqual over Django lost 1,213 of 17,717), because each deeper page ranked a different sample. That is fixed too.

What it does not cover

  • Symbol and reference searches. They have no grep equivalent.
  • Ranking order. That is the ranking-quality suite.
  • API offset paging below 1000 results across a search that hit its budget. It is not stable (see Matching compared with grep and ripgrep). The web UI never pages that way; its LOAD MORE is checked.
  • Queries with more than 20,000 matches under the default limits. Each deep page re-runs the search, so walking them all is slow; raise --defaults-max-hits to include them.
  • The search deadline. The walk runs with no time limit. A search the deadline stops is a sample of whatever was read in time.
  • GNU grep’s test suite. It has not been mined yet.

Search limits

A ranked search cannot rank what it never read, and reading every hit of a word like return in a large repository costs seconds and gigabytes. So each search collects a bounded number of matches, ranks them and shows the best. This page lists those limits, what they cost and what they hide, measured rather than guessed. Every limit that cuts a search is reported with the result (limits_hit), and every one is set in the configuration file.

How it was measured

  • Corpus: Linux, LLVM, Rust and Kubernetes together (shallow clones of their default branches, October 2026): 372,517 files, 607 MB. Common tokens have millions of hits: return 3.08M, struct 3.43M, static 1.26M.
  • Machine: a 4 vCPU cloud VM (Xeon @ 2.1 GHz, 15 GB RAM), warm page cache. Times are engine time, without HTTP. Each figure is the median of 3 runs.
  • Queries: 25, grouped by true hit count: huge (over 100k hits, 10 queries), mid (5k to 100k, 11) and small (under 5k, 4).
  • Reference: each query is first run with no limits at all, giving fcs’s own ranking of every match. A setting is judged by how much of that exhaustive top 50 its page 1 holds (top-50), and in how many queries page 1 starts with the exhaustive #1 (best first). This measures what the limits change, not whether the ranking itself is good. Ties barely affect it: at rank 50 most queries tie with 1 to 20 other hits.

To repeat it on your own code:

cargo run --release --example limits_bench -- <dir>... \
    --index /tmp/fcs-bench.idx --queries queries.txt --json out.jsonl

The first run builds and saves the index, and later runs load it. --only modes,files,budget,page picks the experiments.

Match budget

The budget is how many matches a search collects before it ranks. Before 0.17 a first page collected 562 (a 512 floor). Page 1 was then the best of the first 562 matches found, out of 3.4M for struct:

Grouptop-50best firstmedian / worst
huge10%0 of 1016 / 55 ms
mid11%1 of 118 / 26 ms
small53%2 of 420 / 57 ms

The budget sweep below is for a page of 50. In it, files are read best first by the metadata score, which is how every search reads them now:

Budgethuge: top-50 / best / median / worstmid: top-50 / best / median / worst
51210% / 0 of 10 / 13 / 33 ms18% / 3 of 11 / 7 / 18 ms
2,04810% / 0 / 16 / 36 ms50% / 7 / 21 / 31 ms
8,19236% / 5 / 27 / 47 ms75% / 8 / 49 / 85 ms
16,38443% / 5 / 48 / 74 ms89% / 10 / 77 / 97 ms
32,76881% / 9 of 10 / 78 / 118 ms98% / 11 of 11 / 85 / 162 ms
100,00082% / 9 / 194 / 304 ms98% / 11 / 86 / 145 ms
1,000,00082% / 9 / 2,035 / 2,416 ms98% / 11 / 82 / 154 ms

Small queries reach 100% from 2,048 on. 32,768 is where quality stops improving. Going to a million adds one point and costs 26 times the time. Each collected match takes 182 to 277 bytes while the search runs, so 32,768 is about 7.5 MB per search. If all 64 permitted searches hit it at once, that is about 480 MB.

Defaults: min_match_budget = 32768, match_budget_multiplier = 8 (which only matters for pages ending past 4,096), max_match_budget = 100000. The rest of the huge-query gap is the per-file cap (below) and files with thousands of hits each.

What it costs on a small repository. The sweep above was measured on a large corpus. On tokio 1.45.0 + Django 5.2 (about 6,300 files), the same machine ran examples/corpus_bench at v0.16.2 (budget 512) and v0.17.0 (budget 32,768):

Queryv0.16.2 p50v0.17.0 p50
text, common word4.7 to 5.5 ms54 to 60 ms
text, two characters4.1 to 4.5 ms45 to 47 ms
regex, no literal3.0 to 3.6 ms100 to 108 ms

That is about ten times slower for common words and thirty times for a regex with no literal, and index builds did not change. It buys the quality above: page 1 holds the best hits instead of the first ones found. It was not named in the 0.17.0 changelog; the changelog now records it. Queries with few matches (identifiers, rare words) did not change. Whether the budget can be made cheaper when a repository has few files is an open question.

Reading order

Before 0.17, rank=full, which was auto’s choice below 5,000 candidates, read files in the order they were discovered on disk. At the same budget that order does much worse:

Order, budget 32,768huge: top-50 / bestmid: top-50 / best
best first (now)81% / 9 of 1098% / 11 of 11
discovery order (before)31% / 4 of 1091% / 10 of 11

Discovery order needed about 8 times the budget (262,144, 520 ms median) to come close. Every mode now reads best first. Sorting the candidates costs at most about 14 ms for struct’s 142k candidate files.

Files read (fast_rank_files)

Before 0.17, auto ranking switched to “fast” above 5,000 candidate files and read only the best 2,000. For common words that cost nothing, because the budget fills within the first files read. The cap mattered only for queries with many candidate files (trigram matches) but few real hits: handle_error has 8,046 candidate files and only 173 with a hit.

Files readhits on page 1 (page of 50)top-50time
5002132%11 ms
2,000 (the old default)5054%29 ms
10,000, or no cap50100%104 ms

Neither mode was slow at any candidate count (the worst was 102 ms), so the 5,000 switch protected nothing. Default: no cap (fast_rank_files = 0). A search reads until the budget is spent or the deadline passes. A server may still set a cap, and it is reported as max_files when it bites.

Page size and MCP

Page sizemedian / worst, huge queries
2014 / 38 ms
10013 / 46 ms
50016 / 35 ms
1,000195 / 291 ms

A page of 1,000 gets the 100,000 budget, so it costs more. It was still under 0.3 s for 3M-hit words. MCP’s search_code used to stop at 100 hits with no way to page. Defaults: mcp.default_limit = 50, mcp.max_limit = 1000, plus offset, max_files and rank arguments.

Per-file cap (per_file_matches)

Files with more than 100 hits: return 5,685, struct 7,359, static 1,671, printf 76. The largest single file has 11,242 return hits.

Until 0.17 the cap was 100, applied to a file’s first 100 hits in file order and raised to the page end. Two things were wrong with that. Page 1 showed a busy file’s earliest hits, not its best (a file with 150 long comment lines mentioning a word before 150 short assignments to it showed the comments, score 1.2, and never the assignments, score 7.8). And because the cap grew with the page end, each offset page ranked a different sample: walking such a search by offset, as MCP tells agents to, returned 305 hits of which 155 were distinct.

Now a file keeps its best hits by score, and the cap is the same for every page in a range. Default: 1,000, the end of the range (results before 1000): a file’s 1,001st-best hit can never rank in the first 1,000 results, so the cap leaves out nothing those pages could show. From result 1000 on the cap is the page’s budget, as before. The cost is that every hit of a capped file is scored (the old cap stopped reading the file at hit 100), and a busy file uses up to 1,000 of the budget instead of 100.

Deadline

The slowest default above (a page of 1,000) took 291 ms, and a million-match budget took 2.4 s. The engine deadline, just under request_timeout_secs (30 s), only matters for regex full scans and loaded machines. Default: unchanged.

Caveats

  • One corpus, on four slow cores. Cost follows the matches collected (the budget) more than the repository size. The candidate sort grows with the number of candidate files.
  • Quality is measured against fcs’s own exhaustive ranking, not human judgement.

Scale scoreboard

The other benchmarks time the engine. This one runs the real server binary, starts and stops it, searches it over HTTP from several threads the way people do, and changes the files under it the way branch switches, formatters and builds do. Every scenario reports the same numbers, and a number over its target fails the run.

cargo build --release --bins --example scoreboard
target/release/examples/scoreboard --dir /tmp/fcs-score --files 200000 \
    --targets benches/scoreboard/targets.toml --markdown score.md --json score.json

--scenarios branch-switch,overflow runs some of them, --stream-seconds sets the stream’s length (600 by default). The tree is generated under --dir, 1,000 files to a folder, and reused by later runs (examples/support/load_tree.rs). It is shaped like the real corpus of scripts/bench/ab.sh --corpus real (Linux, LLVM, Rust, Kubernetes; 304,688 files): sizes spread like real files, with a median of 2 KB and a few over 1 MB; 55% of the files in languages fcs extracts symbols from (C, C++, Rust, Go, Python) and the rest IR, assembly, YAML and text; and contents tuned so that indexing costs, per megabyte, about what the real corpus costs. Until 2026-10-25 it was small Python files that all looked alike, which indexed several times faster than real code and hid the costs below.

The report holds numbers only: no paths, queries or file contents. That makes a run on a private tree safe to share. The server’s log and any diagnostic captures stay in --dir.

Scenarios

Each scenario starts the server on its own: from scratch for cold-build, from the saved index for the rest. Searches start at once and go on until the end. The changes start once the restart’s own check of the tree has finished (see Known gaps).

ScenarioWhat it does
cold-buildStart with no saved index; search while it builds.
warm-restartStart from the saved index; search while it loads and checks the tree.
branch-switchRewrite one file in five at once (40,000 at 200,000 files).
streamChange 200 files a second for ten minutes: rewrites, plus a new file and a removed one every 100 ms.
slow-mixEight searchers, plus a regex that reads every file every 3 s, while 50 files a second change for two minutes.
save-under-load500 files a second for two minutes, with the index saved every 5,000 applied changes.
overflowRewrite half the files at once: more events than the watcher’s queue holds (65,536), so it rescans the tree.

The searchers cycle through cheap and expensive queries (task_17_, return value, revision, default_\w+\(\), task_\d+_amber, helpers.default_cobalt) with a page of 50, as the web UI asks.

What is reported

NumberMeaningTarget
turned_awaySearches answered 503 (“index is being updated”)0
refused, errorsNo connection after the server first answered; any other failure0
incompleteAnswered while the index was still being built or checked (the answer says so)reported
p50_ms, p99_ms, max_msSearch time as the client sees it, HTTP includedper scenario
listen_secs, ready_secsFrom start until the server answers anything, and until /api/readyper scenario
fresh_secsFrom the last change until the index matches the disk: nothing pending, and 100 of the changed files checked against the disk with /api/status?paths=per scenario
not_settledThe index never matched the disk within --settle-seconds0
max_pendingThe largest backlog the server reported (sampled every second; the JSON has the curve)reported
rescans“The watched paths will be rescanned” in the server logreported
peak_rss_mb, cpu_secsThe server process’s peak memory and CPU time (Linux)memory per scenario
memory_mbThe index’s estimated heap by structure at the end of the scenario, largest first, so a memory change names the part that grewreported
captures, stall_capturesDiagnostic captures written; the second leaves out slow_search, which an overloaded CPU alone can causestalls 0
crashesThe server exited before it was told to stop0
check_problemsWhat GET /api/check?all=true finds once the index has settled: invariants, every file’s content, the file set against a fresh walk0

The zero targets hold at any size. The others are in benches/scoreboard/targets.toml, set at 1.3 times the worse of two runs (memory: 1.15 times), and apply only to runs with the same number of files. They are today’s numbers, not goals: they are tightened as the indexing-throughput fixes land.

Measured

200,000 files of the realistic tree on a 4 vCPU, 16 GB Linux VM, 2026-10-25 (commit d943516). These are the second of the two runs behind the targets.

ScenarioSearchesIncompletep50p99MaxReadyFresh afterMax pendingPeak RSSStall captures
cold-build1,8351,8351.09 s2.48 s4.17 s618 s––1,177 MB0
warm-restart86791 ms1.78 s2.16 s4.6 s––1,267 MB0
branch-switch436761.82 s4.46 s4.60 s–169 s40,0001,268 MB0
stream1,3491091.96 s4.43 s4.87 s–30.7 s9,7641,556 MB0
slow-mix6282532.48 s8.68 s13.2 s–14.5 s1,6151,454 MB0
save-under-load6551201.93 s4.38 s5.44 s–163 s43,1752,037 MB0
overflow1,0801262.14 s4.65 s6.54 s–496 s132,6842,582 MB1

In every scenario: no search turned away, refused or failed, no crash, and the check found nothing. The server answered within 0.1 s of starting in both start-up scenarios. Searches slower than 5 s wrote slow_search captures in four scenarios, with up to nine searches sharing four CPUs. overflow wrote a backlog capture in both runs, which fails the zero stall_captures target (see below).

On the earlier tree of small Python files (2026-10-23) the same machine built the index in 50 s, caught up with a branch switch in 7 s and kept search p50 under 0.7 s. Nothing got slower in between: the realistic tree holds 1.75 GB where the old one held about 40 MB (200-byte files), and its files cost what real files cost to parse.

What it found

  • Indexing throughput is the bottleneck. With realistic files the watcher applies about 200 to 300 files a second on four CPUs. A 40,000 file branch switch takes about 2.5 minutes to catch up, and rewriting half the tree takes over 8 minutes, long enough for the backlog capture to fire (changes pending for 300 s without draining). That failure is a bug report, not a target to loosen. The plan’s next items are aimed at it: the batch merge runs on one thread (most of a build’s time on the real corpus), and every removed file costs a pass over every posting list.

  • fcs check --live was cut off at 30 s. On a 200,000 file index the full check takes longer than the server’s request timeout, so it answered 504 and the scoreboard counted check problems in four scenarios. /api/check is no longer cut off at the request timeout.

  • A 4–5 s stall after every restart. An index loaded from disk keeps its unresolved imports but not the files’ import lists. At the end of the restart’s check, each of those imports got one last try, all at once and under the write lock: 368,000 imports at 200,000 files, 4 to 5 s during which every search waited. They are now tried in the same short steps as the rest of the import graph (about 250 ms each), and the slowest search after a restart fell from 4.0 s to under 1 s.

  • A backlog capture under a steady stream. The capture fired when changes had been pending for 300 s without a break, which a steady stream of edits always causes, however well the watcher keeps up. It now fires only when the watcher could not have applied what is pending in the last 10 s.

  • lock_wait counted the holding back. A search holds back for a waiting install step by design (up to 2 s, see Incremental updates), and that alone could pass the 2 s threshold. The capture now counts only the time a writer kept the search out.

Known gaps

  • The overflow scenario fails its zero target (one backlog capture) until indexing throughput improves; see above.

  • After a restart, answers say the index is incomplete for a while. Loading takes 2 to 3 s at 200,000 files. After that the server walks the tree to find files that changed while it was down, which took between 4 and 30 s here depending on the search load. Until the walk ends, every answer carries the indexing note, although only files changed since the last run can be missing (incomplete in warm-restart).

  • Changes during the restart’s check. When a large change lands while the restart’s own check still runs, the check’s steps and the watcher’s steps take turns. A search can then wait for one of each: 2.4 s was seen once, with 100,000 files rewritten 3 s after a restart. The scenarios wait for the check to finish before they change files, so this case is not covered by a target yet.

  • Lost events under a stream. One of three ten-minute streams lost events once, about 280 s in: the kernel’s inotify queue (fs.inotify.max_queued_events, 16,384 by default) overflowed. The watcher rescanned the tree and the index matched the disk again within about 15 s, with nothing missing. The cause of the sudden burst is not known yet. rescans is reported, not a target.

  • Only Linux reports memory and CPU. On other systems those columns are empty.

Where it runs

  • Nightly on GitHub (workflow “Scale scoreboard”): 200,000 files with the full ten-minute stream, against the targets. The table goes to the job summary, and the JSON and server logs are kept as an artifact.
  • On pull requests that touch locking, watching, saving, the web server or limits: 50,000 files with a two-minute stream. The zero targets apply; the time and memory targets are only for 200,000 files. A 200,000 file run takes about an hour on the realistic tree.
  • Before a release, on a large machine: a million files (--files 1000000), or your own tree with a sanitised report. The time and memory targets apply only to 200,000 files, so such a run checks the zero targets and records its numbers for comparison with the previous release.

Changelog

All notable changes to this project will be documented in this file.

The format is based on Keep a Changelog, and this project adheres to Semantic Versioning.

Unreleased

Changed

  • A loaded index keeps its posting lists in the index file. The server maps the file and a query decodes only the lists for its own trigrams, so the lists sit in the page cache, which the OS can evict and share, instead of on the heap: at 302,000 files a loaded index holds 6 MB of posting data on the heap instead of about 479 MB (350 MB of the file is mapped instead), and loads 17% faster (4.1 s to 3.4 s, same machine, median of 3). Query times are unchanged. Removing or changing a file costs one bit for the mapped lists instead of a pass over every list. Saves while the server runs (checkpoints) now keep every file’s id, writing a removed file as an empty slot, copy unchanged lists from the mapped file as they are, and leave the server reading the file they wrote; the save at shutdown compacts the slots away. index.memory in /api/diagnostics reports the mapped bytes as mapped_postings_bytes. Index format v11, now v12 (see Fixed; the first start after upgrading rebuilds the index once). Known limitation: on Windows, which cannot replace a mapped file, the lists are still decoded onto the heap at load.
  • Loading a saved index is about 2.5 times faster. With one file changed since the save, a load of the 302,000-file real corpus took 10.3 s and now takes 4.1 s (same machine, median of 5). A load used to renumber the files that survived, which rebuilt every posting list when even one file had changed; each saved file now keeps its id and the changed and deleted ones are tombstoned, as a removal at runtime is. From the same profile: the checksum is computed eight bytes at a time, path tables use a faster hash, directory counts are added once per directory, symbols are moved rather than copied, and the ranking metadata pass runs on every core.
  • Restarts read only the folders that changed. After loading a saved index, the server looked for new files by walking every folder under the roots: at 302,000 files, 2.6 s with the file system cache warm and 9.2 s cold, while answers said the index was incomplete. The last complete walk now saves each folder’s modification time and a fingerprint of its ignore files, and a restart reads only the folders whose time or ignore files changed: 0.18 s warm, 1.3 s cold. A full walk still happens after a configuration change, a change to the global excludes file, or when the index has no stamps yet. Index format v10 (now v12): the first start after upgrading rebuilds the index once. Known limitation: on a file system that does not update a folder’s time when its entries change, a file added while the server was stopped is found only once it or its folder changes again.
  • About 10% less memory on large trees: each file’s path is held once. The ranking metadata, the import resolver’s path tables and the import lists kept their own copies of every path (five to eight per file); they now share the file store’s one copy. On 4 cores the 302,000-file real corpus (Linux, LLVM, Rust, Kubernetes) sits at 1,504 MB resident after a build instead of 1,665 MB, and a loaded Linux kernel index holds 35 MB less heap (468 to 433 MB, counted by allocation). Results are identical. One slowdown, named: a search with a path filter (file:, include/exclude globs) on an index that was just built measured 19% slower on the large corpus (95 to 113 ms), because the paths it reads are spread through the heap; on an index loaded from disk it is as fast as before. Other searches measured 2 to 12% faster.
  • Searches with many candidate files sort only what they read. Every search reads its candidates best first and usually stops at its match budget a thousand or two files in, but it sorted all of them first: at 302,000 candidates, scoring and sorting took 31 to 34 ms. The order is now settled lazily (a selection of the next part, then a sort of just that part), with the same order. Same-machine runs at 302,000 files: short-text search -15%, regex with a literal -10%; other queries within noise.
  • Symbol search about 12 times faster on large trees. It tested every symbol of every file whose text contained the query: for Error on the 302,000-file real corpus (Linux, LLVM, Rust, Kubernetes) that was 5.3 million symbols in 85,000 files, to find the 7,000 files defining a matching name. A dictionary of the distinct case-folded symbol names (1.6 million, one string searched with memmem) now lists the files and symbol kinds behind each name, and a case-insensitive search ranks files from it directly; a case-sensitive one tests only those files. Results are the same. The lines a match is shown on are found without splitting the whole file into lines. Same-machine runs: Error 522 to 42 ms and Req 589 to 42 ms at 302,000 files, 22 to 9 ms and 15 to 6 ms on tokio + Django. What it costs: the dictionary takes memory (63 MB at 302,000 files, 46 MB for the Linux kernel; resident +4%), and it is built after every build on one thread (about 2 s at 302,000 files; on all cores it took half the time but fragmented memory: resident grew 160 MB). It is saved with the index, so a load restores it instead (index format v9: the first start after upgrading rebuilds the index once). Files changed after the dictionary was built are searched symbol by symbol until a sixteenth of the files (at least 4,096) have changed; the update that crosses that line rebuilds it under the write lock.
  • Builds and large watcher batches up to twice as fast. Merging a batch into the index ran on one thread and was half of a build’s time; posting-list inserts were four fifths of that. The posting lists are now split into 64 shards by trigram and each batch is merged shard by shard in parallel. On 4 cores the 302,000-file real corpus (Linux, LLVM, Rust, Kubernetes) builds in 211 s instead of 471 s, tokio + Django in 5.6 s instead of 8.1 s, with the same index and the same memory. Searches measured 0 to 13% slower in same-machine runs, on a just-built and on a reloaded index alike (tokio + Django; up to 19% for one query on the large corpus, from 3 rounds). In one 5-round run the common-word search was slower in every round (+11%); in a second, no query was, and a regex that never touches the changed code moved as much as any. So most of it is noise or code layout, but a small real cost is not ruled out. corpus_bench --loaded-queries now also times the queries on the reloaded index.
  • Less memory after a build or a load (Linux). A build frees far more than the index it keeps (file contents, per-batch trigram sets, merge buffers), and the allocator held on to those pages, so resident memory stayed high until a restart. The server now hands them back to the system once a build or a load is done: tokio + Django went from 135 MB to 107 MB resident, the Linux kernel tree from 793 MB to 709 MB, in 8 and 36 ms, outside the write lock. glibc only; macOS, Windows and the musl build are unchanged.

Added

  • Where the memory goes. /api/diagnostics now reports the index’s estimated heap per structure (index.memory: posting lists, the file table, symbols, references, the dependency graph, imports), largest first, next to the process’s resident memory (index.resident_bytes, Linux). corpus_bench prints the same breakdown as a table, with a “not estimated” row for the allocator’s share, and reports each part as a benchmark metric. The scale scoreboard records it per scenario. A change that grows memory now shows which structure grew. Measured this way, a just-built index holds more than the same index after a restart (tokio + Django: 78 MB against 44 MB of heap), because posting lists built by inserts keep spare capacity.
  • An instruction-count gate on every push to main. The Benchmarks workflow now counts, under callgrind, the instructions each operation takes on tokio + Django (build, every query, an incremental update, save, load) and fails when one takes more than 3% more than at the last passing push (scripts/bench/icount.sh, corpus_bench --icount). Counts barely move between runners, unlike wall time, so added work shows the day it lands. Counts are published only from a passing run, so the run stays red until the work is removed, or accepted with an Accept-instructions: line in the commit message. The Criterion suites no longer run in CI (they still run with cargo bench), and the job takes about as long as before (18 minutes on its first run, 20 before).

Fixed

  • A restart missed a file that had been too large or binary. The restart walk (above) skipped folders whose time had not changed, but a file skipped for its size or contents is not in the load’s checks, and cutting it down rewrites the file, not its folder, so it never joined the index. Each folder stamp now counts the folder’s eligible files, and a folder holding a different number in the index is read again (such folders are read on every restart). Index format v12. Found by the lifecycle model test on main.
  • A regex could take in a CRLF line’s \r. one[^A-Za-z0-9_] matched line one on a line ending in \r\n, where rg --crlf does not: the \r now belongs to the line ending for every pattern, not only $. Found by a generated query in the ripgrep backtest.
  • Saves take a third of the instructions they did. B6’s faster CRC cost more per call, and the save called it for every few bytes bincode wrote, which made saves 7% dearer; the write buffer now sits in front of the CRC, which runs over whole 64 KiB buffers. tokio + Django: 1,269 to 432 million instructions per save (callgrind, one thread).
  • The symbol-search benchmark timed a search no front end runs. The library’s search_symbols (used by the corpus benchmark, the validator and tests) ran an older single-pass search; the web API, gRPC, MCP and fcs run the two-phase one. search_symbols now runs that one too, so the published symbol numbers are what a user waits for: on the 302,000-file corpus about 510 ms for an exact name and 580 ms for a prefix, not the 350 ms the benchmark showed (tokio + Django: 19 and 13 ms, not 13 and 8). Nothing a user runs got slower; the benchmark now measures it. Making these faster is the next item (a symbol name index).
  • fcs check --live --all-content was cut off on a large index. The server’s whole-request timeout (request_timeout_secs, 30 s) applied to /api/check, so a check that read every file of a large tree answered 504 while the client was ready to wait an hour. The check runs one at a time and only reads, so it is no longer timed out. Found by the scale scoreboard on a 200,000-file tree with real-sized files.
  • Folders that could not be read went missing without a word. When the build, a restart’s check or a rescan could not list a folder (permission denied, an I/O error), it logged a debug line and skipped it, so its files were missing from every search. Each such folder is now logged as a warning and counted with every search: “1 folder could not be read, so the files in it are missing from the index”, in the web UI (“1 folder unreadable”), fcs, MCP, /api/status and fcs check --live. The count clears when a later scan (a restart, a rescan) can read the folder.
  • Folders the watcher could not watch were not reported. A folder the file watcher could not list was dropped silently, and one it could not watch (the OS watch limit) was only logged. Both are now counted while watching (“2 folders not watched”): changes in them reach the index only at the next rescan or restart.
  • A file-watcher error now triggers a rescan. An error from the OS watcher (its event stream failed) was logged and nothing else, so the events it lost were lost for good. The watched folders are now reconciled with the disk, at most once every five minutes so a repeating error cannot keep a rescan of a large tree running.

Documentation

  • Benchmark numbers in the README, the book and the site are rendered, not typed. They come from one pinned Benchmarks run (docs/bench/published.json), now the run for c635718, which includes the faster build: tokio + Django builds in 4.9 s instead of 5.5 s. CI fails when a page and the pinned run disagree. The file-format page said format v7; the index has been v8 since v0.16.0.
  • Benchmark numbers now match what fcs does. The README, the book’s benchmark pages and the project site showed numbers from 8 September (before v0.17.0), with a common word at about 1 ms. They now show the v0.19.0 run: a common word over tokio + Django is 24.6 ms at p50, an identifier 7.8 ms, and a query with no match 0.6 µs (since refreshed from a later run, see above).
  • The v0.17.0 slowdown, named. v0.17.0 raised the match budget from 512 to 32,768, so a search ranks many more matches before it returns page one. On a ~6,300-file repository that made common-word text searches about 10 times slower (about 5 ms to about 55 ms on the same machine) and regexes with no literal about 30 times slower (about 3 ms to about 100 ms). It buys ranking quality: page one now holds 81% of the exhaustive top 50 instead of 10%. Queries with few matches and index builds did not change. See the book’s Search limits page.
  • The release run-book in docs/DEVELOPMENT.md now matches how releases are cut, and the instruction files say which docs repeat which numbers so they are updated together.

0.19.0 - 2026-10-24

Searches keep running while the watcher applies changes, the index checks itself and survives crashes and full disks, and fcs gains diff impact (fcs impact, MCP diff_impact) and diagnostic captures. The web UI’s Ranking setting is gone: every search is ranked the same way.

Upgrading: no re-index and no config change is needed. The background scrub ([diagnostics] scrub) is on by default and reads about 3,000 files a minute; set scrub = false to turn it off. fcs --rank and rank in the REST and gRPC APIs still work but no longer change the order with default settings. Release binaries are about 3 MB larger because they keep function names for readable stacks.

Changed

  • The web UI’s Ranking setting (Auto, Fast, Full) is gone. With the default settings all three gave the same results; only a server with a file limit (search.fast_rank_files) told them apart. Every search is ranked the same way, and BEST MATCHES FIRST beside the result count now opens a short explanation of the order: how hits are scored, that a file’s hits stay together, that nothing is hidden, and what happens on a very broad search. MCP’s search_code no longer lists rank (an old client’s value is accepted and ignored) and says how results are ranked in its description. fcs --rank is hidden but still works, as does rank in the REST and gRPC APIs. The book’s Ranking page has the measurements behind the choice: stopping at the first page is up to 50 times faster on tokio + Django, but for common words that page holds none of the 50 best hits.
  • lock_wait captures leave out the holding back. A search holds back while an install step waits (up to 2 s, as long as recent searches take), and that alone could pass the 2 s threshold. The capture now counts only the time a writer kept the search out, so it still means a step or a save held the lock longer than it should.
  • rdeps: and deps: filters no longer read every indexed path to find their file (when it is given as results print it, with or without :N) nor copy every unresolved import to count the broken ones. On a 348,000-file index the filter’s own cost fell from 464 ms to 0.1 ms median (examples/rdeps_timing.rs); a partial path or a folder still reads every path.
  • The server accepts request bodies up to 8 MiB (was 64 KiB), for diffs.

Fixed

  • Building the index got about 30% slower over October, mostly for nothing. A profile on tokio + Django put 42% of the build’s CPU in Python import resolution and another fifth of Django’s in filtering Python references, not in parsing. For every absolute import (most of them the standard library), resolution built and normalized candidate paths under every folder above the importing file; it now asks one folder lookup whether anything is indexed under root/<first name> before building any. The reference filter looked each reference up from the root of the syntax tree and climbed with a call that itself walks down from the root; it now walks each file’s tree once. Results are unchanged (same 9,784 graph edges and 384,986 references; the new filter keeps exactly what the old one kept for all 492,103 identifiers in Django). Import resolution went from 2.4 s to 0.85 s, the whole build from 8.1 s to 6.7 s on the same machine (0.15.2: 6.1 s; the rest is the extra Python references and case folding). The corpus benchmark now reports the time of each build phase.
  • A search for a common word spent a third of its time sorting hits it never returned. Every hit (22,000 for return on tokio + Django) was sorted, then sorted again by file, to return one page; and each candidate file’s src/test path signals were worked out again on every search. Now each file’s best hit places the file, only the files the page reaches have their hits sorted, and the signals come from the file’s precomputed metadata. Results are identical (53,810 results compared across 14 queries, both ranking modes and pages down to offset 20,000); return went from 51 to 39 ms, a filtered search from 28 to 21 ms, a regex with no literal from 76 to 61 ms.
  • The count of changes still to apply counted events, not files. One save raises several file-system events, and each was counted, so the web page’s “N file changes to apply” (and the same count in fcs, MCP and /api/status) overstated the backlog: 372,534 for 250,000 changed files in a 500,000-file scale run. Each file now counts once until its change is applied.
  • class ModelForm put class ModelFormOptions: first. Since plain searches match like rg -F (0.15.2), a query is also found inside longer names, and the shorter line won. A whole-word match now scores 1.5x, so the line that has what was typed comes first; the ranking benchmark is back to 20 of 20 at rank 1.
  • The benchmark history stopped on 13 Oct 2026. The ranking check in the Benchmarks workflow failed from that release on, and its failure skipped the steps that store the numbers, so the trend page stopped without a warning. The numbers are now stored first and the check fails the run after.
  • Loading an index with one changed file took about 70% longer. When any saved file had changed or gone, loading rebuilt every trigram posting list one id at a time through a hash lookup. Now that a file saved within seconds of an edit is re-read on load (below), the corpus benchmark hit this on every run, as does a restart soon after an edit. The lists are now rebuilt in one sorted pass from a lookup table, in parallel: on tokio + Django, load after 20 edits went from about 560 ms to 365 ms (a clean load is about 310 ms). Saves after deletes use the same code.
  • Every save killed half way left a file as large as the index. A save writes a temp file next to the index and renames it into place; a server killed during the save (kill -9, a power cut, the OOM killer) left the temp file, and nothing removed it. The server now removes them when it starts.
  • A save that failed was forgotten. With save_after_updates, the count of unsaved updates was reset whether the save worked or not, so after a save onto a full disk the shutdown skipped its save and the saved index lacked those updates (the next start re-read the files, so no search was wrong, but it took longer). The count now stays until a save works.
  • A failed rename could delete the index. When moving the new index into place failed, the old index was deleted before trying again, so a second failure left no index at all. The old index is now moved aside and put back if the second try fails.
  • A panic while reading a file left it out of the index without a word. During the build, a file whose reading or parsing panicked was skipped and not reported. It is now tried again at the end of the build, and reported as unreadable if it keeps failing, like a file that cannot be read.
  • Every search waited 4 to 5 s soon after a restart. An index loaded from disk keeps its unresolved imports (import os, packages outside the indexed folders) but not each file’s import list. At the end of the restart’s check of the tree, each of those imports got one last try, all at once under the index lock: 368,000 of them on a tree of 200,000 files. They are now tried in short steps like the rest of the import graph; the slowest search after a restart fell from 4.0 s to under 1 s.
  • The backlog diagnostic capture fired under steady edits. It fired when changes had been pending for 300 s without a break, which a steady stream of edits always causes. It now fires only when the watcher could not have applied what is pending in the last 10 s.
  • The watcher could fall far behind while people searched. While an install step waits for the index, new searches hold back so the running ones can finish and the step gets in. They held back a fixed 250 ms, so with searches longer than that a new one got in before the running ones ended and the step kept waiting: in a simulation with four people running half-second searches back to back, a change that takes 5 s to install took up to 62 s. A search now holds back for one and a half times the 90th percentile of recent searches’ length (250 ms to 2 s); the same change takes about 10 s, and quick searches still wait only 250 ms behind one slow one. The docs claimed “half speed or better” regardless; the book’s “Incremental updates” page now has the table.
  • The watcher could index files the build leaves out, by ignore rules. When a file changed, the watcher decided whether it was ignored differently from the walk that builds the index (and from ripgrep): a rule deeper down (!a/b/ after /a) let files back in from a folder that is ignored; the nearest ignore file decided, where a parent’s .ignore or .rgignore rule should beat a .gitignore next to the file; and rules in folders above a nested repository were not applied inside it. Such files became searchable after an edit and disappeared again at the next restart. The check now follows ripgrep’s rules, and both are compared with rg --files on random trees of ignore files.
  • Folders ignored by a parent’s .gitignore stayed out only until something happened in them. With sub/ in the root .gitignore, the watcher indexed everything in pkg/sub when the folder was created, renamed in, had an ignore file written in it, or was rescanned after lost events: a walk never checks whether the folder it starts from is ignored. It now does.
  • A restart re-indexed changed files that are now ignored. A file that changed while the server was down was re-read without checking the ignore rules (only the size cap and excludes), so a file a new .gitignore rule covered came back if it had also changed. Changed files now go through the same rules as discovery.
  • A file rewritten within the same second could stay stale. On file systems with coarse timestamps (FAT, HFS+, some network shares), a file rewritten with the same size in the same tick it was indexed kept its old content in the index across a restart or a rescan after lost events, because its size and time had not changed. A file read within 3 seconds of being modified is now read again by the next rescan or restart, as git does with “racily clean” files.
  • Short queries missed very small files. A file shorter than three bytes with a name shorter than three letters (x.py holding ab) has no trigram, and queries without a trigram of their own (two characters, a regex with no literal) scanned only files some posting list named, so rg found the line and fcs did not. The index now keeps the set of all its files, after a load and incremental changes too.
  • indexer.extra_binary_extensions works. The matching reference documented it, but the configuration rejected it as an unknown key. Every file walk and eligibility check now takes its settings from one place.
  • Rewriting a large file while it was searched could crash the server. Files over 1 MB were memory-mapped, and when another program truncated and rewrote one while a search read it, the server died with SIGBUS. Files are now read into memory when searched, so nothing can change under a search. The 3-second “vm.max_map_count is very low” pause at startup is gone with the mapping.
  • No more “server unavailable” at the end of a large build or while a saved index loads. Resolving the dependency graph at the end of a build held the write lock for seconds (about 6 s on 300,000 files), and loading a saved index held it for the whole load, so searches were turned away. Both now work outside the lock and hold it only for short steps or the final swap; a 300,000-file cold build and restart answered every search.
  • Ignore files behave like ripgrep’s. .rgignore is honoured, and when a .gitignore, .ignore or .rgignore changes (a branch switch, say) the watcher re-checks the files under it, so newly ignored files leave the index and newly allowed ones join it. Before, each file kept its old state until it changed itself.
  • A file that can’t be read is retried and reported, not silently dropped. A read that failed (a file locked by an editor or virus scanner, permissions changing mid-save) removed the file from the index until it next changed, and the initial build left such a file out without a word. The watcher now keeps the old entry and retries for about half a minute; then the file is left out and every search says how many files could not be read. A panic while applying watcher changes used to skip the whole batch; the batch is now applied again, and a change that fails twice is reported the same way.
  • A large change no longer leaves the index “updating” for minutes while people search. Each watcher step got the lock only after the running searches finished and then installed just 512 files, so with searches running the watcher applied a few hundred files a second. On a 200,000-file tree, 100,000 rewritten files were still 17,000 behind four minutes later. A step now stays in about as long as it waited, up to one second; the same change is applied 10 s after it stops, and on a million-file tree 200,000 changed files catch up in about 3.5 minutes, with no search turned away. examples/watch_load.rs now checks that the index matches the disk again after the load.
  • Searches no longer stop while the file watcher applies many changes. One slow search plus any watcher step used to stop every other search until the slow one finished, and those still waiting after five seconds got “Index is currently being updated”. The watcher (and the startup build) now take the write lock only in a moment when no search holds it, so a search waits for a short install step at most, never for other searches. On a million-file tree with 50,000 files rewritten at once, searches turned away fell from 3% to none and the slowest search from 5.1 s to 1.5 s, while the watcher applied 5.6 times as many changes in the same time. Measurements are in the book (How it works > Incremental indexing).

Added

  • More ways the tests look for wrong answers. A model-based test (tests/lifecycle_model.rs) drives the index through random sequences of edits, renames, folder moves, ignore-file changes, lost events, saves, restarts and crashes, and after every step compares it with a naive model, page by page; it found the first two fixes above. Property tests (tests/search_properties.rs) compare literal and regex searches with ripgrep on random text full of case-folding oddities (ß, ſ, the Kelvin sign, the sigmas, dotted and dotless i). A generator of seeded Python codebases with an answer key (tests/python_codebase.rs) checks the import graph, symbol and reference searches and strict -deps/-rdeps scopes, after a build and after the watcher rewrites the tree; grimp and Python check the key itself. Book page: How it works > How fcs is tested.
  • Every surface walked to the last hit, and the first page pinned. tests/contract_walk.rs runs searches with thousands of hits through REST, gRPC, MCP (HTTP and fcs mcp), fcs and the web page in headless Chromium, pressing LOAD MORE and “Show N more hits” until they are gone, and every surface must show the same hits in the same order with honest counts. tests/rank_snapshot.rs keeps the first page of about a hundred searches in a file, so a change that reorders results shows in its pull request. The ripgrep backtest also runs on an index restarted from disk and on one the watcher filled (--history reload|watch), with regexes made up from the corpus (--generate-regex), and tests/discovery_oracle.rs compares the indexed files and the FILES box with rg.
  • The import graph is checked on monorepo layouts. Generated repositories shaped like real ones (scripts/graph-oracle/layouts.py, ts_layouts.mjs): Python projects side by side each with its own src/, a root folder that is a package, a namespace package split over two folders, and npm workspaces with tsconfig paths and extends, barrels and .mts/.cts. grimp and TypeScript give the answer. Every edge fcs misses or draws wrongly, there and on Django and Vite, is now a known gap listed with its reason and exact count in benches/graph/known_gaps.toml, instead of a count to stay under. The Python oracle (fcs check included) now builds one graph across ROOT and ROOT/src, so imports from one into the other are compared too.
  • Kill and restart. examples/chaos.rs kills the server binary with SIGKILL at random moments (during a build, a load from disk, a stream of edits, a periodic save, a burst of changes, and the save at shutdown), starts it again and checks that it loads the saved index, leaves no temp file, catches up with every change and passes /api/check. The “Scale scoreboard” workflow runs it nightly and on pull requests that touch saving or watching (job “Kill and restart”).
  • More fault points (--features failpoints, src/faults.rs): out of file handles, a file gone between listing and reading, a slow disk, a panic while reading or parsing a file, events lost by the OS, a save onto a full disk and a failed rename, each with a test in tests/fault_injection.rs.
  • A scale scoreboard. examples/scoreboard.rs runs the server binary over HTTP in seven scenarios (cold build, warm restart, branch switch, a ten-minute stream of edits, overloaded searching, saves under load, and an event-queue overflow) and reports the same numbers for each: searches turned away, latency, time until the index matches the disk, memory, CPU, diagnostic captures, crashes and what /api/check finds. Targets are in benches/scoreboard/targets.toml; the “Scale scoreboard” workflow runs it at 200,000 files nightly and on pull requests that touch locking or watching. Book page: Benchmarks > Scale scoreboard.
  • The lock protocol on a simulated clock. fast_code_search::sim runs threads in turns on simulated time, and the engine lock and the watcher’s install steps read and sleep through it, so tests/lock_sim.rs runs the real code under minutes of made-up search load in about a second, the same on every run and OS. It found the watcher fix above, and its tuning table is the evidence for the hold-back and MAX_HOLD values.
  • The index checks itself. fcs check --live asks the running server whether its index agrees with itself, whether indexed files still match the disk, and whether it holds the files a fresh walk finds. It prints counts only, so the output is safe to share (--show-paths for file names); GET /api/check returns the same as JSON. A background scrub ([diagnostics] scrub, on by default) reads 3,000 files a minute, about 0.1% of a core. It logs and re-indexes any file the index has out of step, which is a lost change, and checks the index’s own consistency hourly; the counts are on the diagnostics page. The watch, fault and backtest harnesses now run the same checks after every scenario. Book page: Guides > Checking a live index.
  • Fault-injection tests. A failpoints cargo feature compiles in named fault points (src/faults.rs) that tests arm to make a read fail or a step panic; tests/fault_injection.rs uses them to check that the build and the watcher retry and report failures. CI runs them on every platform.
  • Diagnostic captures. With [diagnostics] capture = true the server notices when it is slow or stuck and writes what it was doing to a JSON file: a search slower than 5 s or kept waiting over 2 s for the index lock, the watcher or index build making no progress for 120 s, a search still running after 120 s, the index lock never free, or a watcher backlog lasting 300 s. A capture holds the trigger, the search’s phase timings, the searches running, the index and watcher state, recent watcher bursts, memory and threads, the last log lines and, for a hang, every thread’s state and stack (gdb or eu-stack when installed). Query text, paths and file names are left out unless include_queries = true. Captures are rate-limited and capped (50 files, 100 MB). fcs diagnose [DIR] summarizes them, and /api/diagnostics says where they go. Off by default; thresholds are measured (watch_load --capture-dir). See the book’s “Diagnostic captures” page. Release binaries now keep function names (about 3 MB larger) so thread stacks and panic backtraces are readable.
  • Diff impact: fcs impact, POST /api/impact, MCP diff_impact. Give it a diff (by default your uncommitted changes) and it lists, for each function, class, method, type or constant the diff changes, the files that use it, best first, with the use lines: finer than the file-level change_impact. Functions and classes are traced through importers that name them (renames followed), methods and fields by name among every importer with files naming the class first. Each use is a recorded reference, a code mention or a text mention (strings and comments). On Django’s 248 most recent commits that change Python, checked against Jedi, it lists 100% of the files that use a changed function or class (79.5% of those it lists are confirmed by Jedi), in 49 ms median per commit. No re-index is needed. See the book’s Diff impact page for what it misses.
  • Search responses report file changes the watcher has seen and not applied yet (index.pending_changes). The web UI shows “Index updating · N file changes to apply”; fcs and the MCP tools print it as a note.

0.18.1 - 2026-10-16

Small fixes to piped fcs paths and MCP totals. No re-index or config change is needed.

Fixed

  • Piped fcs output and fcs -l print short paths relative to the current folder even when the configuration lists a root as a relative path or with .. in it (they were long but still valid paths).
  • MCP search_code says “total not known” instead of “the scan stopped early” when it has no exact total; the note under it gives the reason (for example, the index is still being built).

0.18.0 - 2026-10-15

Fixes from the 2026-10-15 review of what fcs promises against what it does. Paging is now consistent, busy files show their best hits, several silent cut-offs now say so, and the server keeps its index current by default.

Upgrading: file watching is now on by default. A config without a watch key now watches its folders; add watch = false under [indexer] to keep the old behaviour. No re-index is needed. A file with many matches now shows its best-scoring hits rather than its first ones, so result order changes for broad searches. search.per_file_matches now defaults to 1,000 (was 100). Files that were skipped as binary only because of control characters, and SVG files, are now indexed, as ripgrep searches them.

Changed

  • watch defaults to true. The server follows edits, renames and deletes unless the config says watch = false, matching what the README and guides describe. --init writes watch = true. The build/ and dist/ default excludes stay, and remain listed as a difference from ripgrep in the matching reference.

Fixed

  • Offset paging no longer repeats or skips hits. The per-file cap and match budget grew with the page’s end, so page two could be ranked from a different set than page one (an MCP walk of 305 hits returned 155 distinct ones). Both are now constant below result 1,000 and constant per page beyond it, so consecutive pages join up.
  • A busy file shows its best hits. The per-file cap kept a file’s first N matches in line order; it now keeps its N best by score.
  • Searches during index build say so. Results carry a new indexing limit (web UI: “index still building”; MCP and fcs notes) and no exact-looking total.
  • fcs piped and fcs -l no longer stop silently at 50. -l pages through every hit and lists every matching file; other output prints a stderr note when more hits exist, with the --offset for the next page. Piped and -l paths are relative to the current folder when the configuration’s roots are known, so fcs -l foo | xargs $EDITOR opens the right files.
  • Two indexed folders with the same name no longer collide. Each root gets a distinct label (extended with parent folders when names clash), so the viewer opens the right file.
  • Go to definition with watch off no longer jumps to a line that no longer holds the symbol; a cached definition is used only if its name is still on that line.
  • Symbol search totals are honest. When a file limit leaves files unread, the total is shown as unknown and the max_files limit is reported; full ranking reads every file.
  • Ranking no longer depends on where the repository sits on disk. The src/, lib/ and test signals use the root-relative path.
  • Test files are demoted by their names too (_test.go, .spec.ts, .test.js, __tests__/, test_*.py and similar), not only by folder.
  • Ignore rules match ripgrep: .git/info/exclude and the global gitignore (core.excludesFile) are honoured by indexing and watch.
  • Binary detection matches ripgrep: a file is binary only if its first 8 KB contains a NUL byte. SVG is no longer on the binary extension list.
  • case: and word: accept only yes/no style values; anything else is searched as text. A query that holds only filters now explains how to add text to find.
  • The index size statistic counts indexed bytes, not files on disk.
  • The web UI no longer merges Foo.ts and foo.ts into one group.

Documentation

  • The book’s matching, search limits, memory and ranking pages and the CLI guide describe the new behaviour and every remaining difference from ripgrep.

0.17.0 - 2026-10-15

Search limits are measured, raised and configurable. Page 1 of a search for a common word now holds most of what an unlimited search would rank first, and MCP agents can page through every hit.

Upgrading: no re-index is needed. Page 1 of a broad search now ranks different (better) hits than 0.16.x. If your config sets server.fast_rank_files, server.max_fast_rank_files or server.max_page_offset, move them to the new [search] section; the old keys still work but log a deprecation warning.

Changed

  • Page 1 ranks far more of a broad query’s matches. A first page collected 512 matches before ranking (the “match budget”); it now collects 32,768. On a 372k-file corpus (Linux, LLVM, Rust, Kubernetes) page 1 held 10% of the exhaustive top 50 for words with over 100k hits, and now holds 81%, at about 80 ms median. Each collected match costs about 230 bytes while the search runs, about 7.5 MB per search at the new floor. The measurements are on the book’s new “Search limits” page.
  • Every search reads files best first. Files are read in order of the per-file score fast ranking used, for every mode, instead of in discovery order below 5,000 candidate files. The budget therefore keeps the hits of the most likely files. Ordering side effect: with the bigger budget and the new order, page 1 of a broad search shows different (better ranked) hits than before; the result list is still plain rank order, and no hit is hidden.
  • No more 2,000-file stop. Above 5,000 candidate files, auto ranking read only the best 2,000 and stopped, even when those files held too few hits to fill the page, so a rare string in a big repository could come back nearly empty. Searches now read until the budget is spent or the deadline passes. A server can still set a cap (search.fast_rank_files), reported as max_files as before. rank=full never stops at a file cap; auto and fast honour one.
  • MCP search_code returns 50 hits by default (was 20), allows up to 1,000 (was 100), takes offset, max_files and rank, and lists 20 files whose path matches (was 5). When more hits exist it says the offset of the next page.

Added
